DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

PRINCESS SALM-SALM.

One of the most beautiful and distinguished figures in the great London world is the Prin- cess Salm-Salm, who is actnally the King of Spain's first cousin. Both she and the Prince were much in evidence this season, and they gave a number of delightful dinner-parties. Naturally, too, says the Sketch, they enjoyed every moment of the Cowes Week, for the Princess is an enthusiastic yachtswoman—in- deed, to her belongs the honour of having in- vented the smart yachting-cap so much in vogue which is raised at one side by a jaunty little tartan bow. Princess Salm-Salrn was born an Austrian Archduchess, being the eldest daughter of the Archduke Frederick, who is tho brother of the Queen-Dowager of Spain. Some four years ago she married at Vienna Prince Emmanuel Salm-Salm, a gallant, good- looking young cavalry officer belonging to the senior branch of that ancient family, which, being mediatised, is of rank equal to that of a reigning Royal Family. They have two beauti- ful little daughters, Princess Isabelle and Prin- cess Rosemary, both born at Potsdam, for their father holds a commission in the crack Prussian Regiment of Guar'ds.

A PRETTY PLOESE.

The original little blouse portrayed was 'in- tended for realisation in tussore silk, but it would reproduce quite prettily in such inexpen- sive materials as delaine, cotton voile, or even some of the finer makes of French flannel. The little tucked yoke may be part of the blouse

itself, or composed of some transparent fabric, and worn as a separate under-yoke. The straps are formed of the self material, the insertion holes being worked in buttonhole stitch, and run through with soft satin ribbon, which is tied into rosettes over the shoulders, at the elbow, and on the yoke.

WHY Gillis WED Itlons.

I have given the subject mature consideration, says "A Spinster" in M. A. P., and have come to the conclusion tbat they do it, against their will and inclination, for two or three mordant reasons. In the first place, the power of the getter-on exercises upon them a fearful fascina- tion. They feel the self-contained determination under the tough's dull appearance, and it com- pels them. For money opens every gate, and ' woman, being quickwitted even in her girlhood, realises this keenly. She knows that marriage with a getter-on means a brilliant future for her --a definite position in society, all the beautiful delightful things she reads about in books—and it is impossible that this prospect should not in- fluence the most innocent and undesigning of -young damsels. Her parents, relations, and fends are strenuous in advising her to make the match, which sho is well aware many other girls are eager to snatch from her. And so in- clination, instinct, romance, and all the wondrous fancies that lurk in her heart are sacrificed at one fell swoop. No one but a woman knows what the sacrifice involves. No man, I think, could- possibly realise the loathing, flesh-crawling horror we experience at the touch of a man who cannot rouse in us either our affection or that mysterious affinite de chair which is so great a part of love. Fortunately, the tough is gener- ally an inaifferent lover; his caresses are half- hearted, his blood being cooled by the exercise of constant calculation. And, of course, one can get used to everything in time. But any girl who has once been kissed by a man she does not like will do well to think twice before she marries in defiance of the little organ she calls her heart.

CHRISTIAN CIRCLE STORIES.

So much harm is done by trashy literature of the "Penny Dreadful" order with which the market has been flooded during the last few years, that it is quite refreshing to welcome "Christian Circle Stories," the first number of which is now on sale at all newsagents and bookstalls. "Mid London's Glare "is the title of No. 1, and it is really a delightful story by that ever popular author, Sydney Watson. It is crammed with human interest from beginning to end, and we imagine that any of our readers who begin this romance of love and pathos will follow the characters with keen interest to the last page. It is a very fascinating story of high moral tone, very suitable for family reading, and yet is devoid of anything of the" goody goody" order. There is also a serial by Lilias Campbell Davidson, and when we remember that this penny weekly consists of 24 pages, well printed and splendidly illustrated, we wonder how it is done for the money, and at any rate we predict for "Christian Circle Stories " a large and ready sale.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Continental Spas.—With the departure of King Edward, the season at Marienbad comes to an end. The famous Austrian spa, has been crowded with British and American visitors, and His Majesty's patronage of the place year after year, has done much to popularise it with those classes who delight to go where Royalty goes. It is consequently much more given over to the gay and fashionable element of society, and the real invalids prefer Carlsbad, or other Spas. where the "cure "is taken more seriously. At Marienbad, the King has his early morning walk a little before eight, ' when he takes the waters, but at Carlsbad the visitors are expected to make their pilgrimage to the springs shortly after sunrise—and enjoy the open air concert from six to eight o'clock. There is the fashionable evening promenade, but as a rule more attention is paid at Carlsbad to the three golden rules of the "cure," viz, early hours, walking and simple diet, than at Marienbad. The Queen in Norway.—Queen Alexandra and Princess Victoria are expected back from their visit to Norway and Denmark about the middle of the month, and will join the King at Balmoral, where their Majesties will reside until October sth or 6th. The Queen. who is very poplar among the Norwegians, has visited Norway on two or three former occasions, but that beautiful country naturally presents a greater interest for her since Princess Maud became its Queen. The Queen and Princess Victoria have been spending part of the time on board the Royal Yacht, visiting the chief places of interest. Court life at Christiania is of an even more simple character than at Copenhagen, but that is what our Queen most appreciates, and it has been of the greatest enjoyment to her to spend the time with her daughter and son-in-law, in their new sphere and surroundings. Later in the autumn King llaakon, with Queen Maud, and their son Olaf, are to pay a visit to the King and Queen at Windsor. Appleton Hall, their Norfolk home is still kept up, but now that Princess Maud is one of the reigning Queens of Europe, she will not be able to spend as much time as she would like in this country. The Death of Lady Campbell-Bannerman.—The late Lady Henry Campbell-Bannerman was, like her husband, of pure Scottish descent. The daughter of General Sir Charles Bruce, she married'the then Mr. Henry Campbell in 1860, as it was subsequent to his marriage that the Premier took his second name under the will of his maternal uncle. There are no children of the marriage, and for many years before her death Lady Henry had been an invalid, which prevented her from taking a very active part in social life. She had only been out once or twice since the Premier took up his residence in Downing-street, but although in a precarious state, she looked forward hopefully to a return in better health from her visit to Marienbad, where she had been in the habit of going for very many years. She was not one to court the public gaze, but, like the late Mrs. W. E. Gladstone, she took the keenest interest in her husband's affairs and political career, and it was a grief to her that she was unable to take a more active part in the duties and hospitalities which devolve upon his high office, or that she had no daughter to take her place. Winchester Cathedral.—Visitors to Winchester Cathedral this slimmer will not be surprised at the official statement that it is in peril of becoming a ruin, although it will be nothing short of a national calamity if tke necessary funds are not forthcoming to save it from such a fate—St. Swithin, who lived and died in the ninth century, and is supposed still to influence our weather, was the seventeenth Bishop of Winchester,but the old Saxon Cathedral was replaced by a Norman one. It was commenced by Bishop Walkelin, a kinsman of the Conqueror, and this building forms a substantial part of the present Cathedral. According to the story, the King granted the Bishop as much wood from the Forest of Hempage, as he could cut down in four days and nights, but he did not limit the number of wood- men, and the Bishop gathered together a small army, and cut down the whole forest. In the year 1107, the centre tower fell, becaussa it was said, the ungodly William Rufus was interred in the Cathedral, and many alterations in the original edifice have naturally been effected from time to time. Still it ranks as one of the finest examples of early Norman architecture, and, with exception of that of Canterbury,is the longest cathedral in England. Another Stage Peerage Romance.—Miss Camille Clifford, whose engagement to the Hon. Henry Lyndhurst Bruce has excited so much interest, is known - chiefly on this side of the Atlantic for the part she played as a Gibson Girl in " The Prince of Pilson," and subsequently in " The Catch of the Season." In the latter company was also another Gibson Girl, Miss Eva Carrington, who married Lord de Clifford last February and a few days after Lord Ashburton married Miss Frances Donnelly, so that there is a prospect of a third stage and peerage wedding taking place this year. Miss Clifford, although representing the American girl on the stage, is a Norwegian by birth, and the Hon. Henry Lyndhurst Bruce, who has been singularly free and frank in talking about his matrimonial and bvsitiess affairs, is the eldest scn of Lord Aberdeen, and is now in his twenty-sixth year. The Corselet Skirt.—Very few corselet skirts are to be seen now, and bearing in mind the exhibitions that have fallen to our share during the summer, we cannot regret this state of affairs. Built by leading tailors, and given a perfect figure, the corselet skirt was just passable though never pretty or becoming in the hands of inexperienced cutters, and makers it was the most hideous device kneel nahle.The princess robe we have still with us, but there are rumours to the effect that with the autumn it will disappear for a while,and that Louis XIV. and Louis XVII. styles will reign supreme. A New Trimming.—ln the world of trimmings selvyt is to the fore, a fabric which combines the best qualities of leather and suede, while it possesses much of the charm of velvet. It is impossible to crease it, end to rain it is impervious. Tailors are building entire costumes, and motor coats of selvyt the colours so far preferred being dull olive greens, and chestnut browns. Comparatively few of us will be able to indulge in so costly a toilette, but collars, lapels, cuffs, and perchance vests of selvyt will not spell ruin to the majority of us. The Care of the Teeth.—Many people who would naturally possess quite a tolerable set of natures " wories " spoil them by carelessness, and ruin their digestion by omitting to consult a dentist when the first symptoms of decay make their appearance. As a matter of fact, a visit should be paid every six months, for the purpose of ascertaining if any attention is rcquired, and this should be especially observed in the case of small children, who may thereby be saved an infinity of pain and trouble, not to speak of laying the seeds of an impaired digestion in after life. One of the first things a child should be taught is the use of A toothbrush—a soft one, which will not injure the tender gums, while the water should he warm instead of cold. It should be pointed out to the beginner too, that the inner ide of the teeth require quite as much attention as thg outer, and that an up and down movement is better than one across the teeth and gums. Good teeth are a rare enough possession nowadays to be noticeable, and one to be carefully and assiduously cultivated. MA DOE

Saturday, September 8, 1906

T-':-'IE FIE OPAL.

By H. de Vere Stacpoole.

===== 0002601_19060908_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
.fiuthor of" The Doctor," "Fanny Lambert," etc. ~fj

CHAPTER Xli

ATE left Daisy—aTal;m—lua--aws she had hired a boy to carry her gladstone bap She was going she did not know where., Nothing but youth and the inexperience born of youth could have led her into such a fateful and, one might say, terrible position. A looker-on from the outside might have con- sidered the act of leaving her aunt's house an impulsive one. It was not in the least sod For years the instinct of rebellion against the ty-rrany of her aunt had been growing in her.• The position she held in her house had been growing more and more insupportable, a creature kept to be nagged at, to be reproved, to be ordered and bullied in a way that no servant would submit to, this had been her part since leaving school. She had submitted, possessed of a naturally sweet temper, knowing that her position in her aunt's house was one of a dependent. She had submitted and Miss Princeps took her submission for a sign of timidity and weakness of character.

The happenings of last night, especially the blow to her love for Arthur, had, however, broken down the barriers; all the pent up re- sentment in the girl's breast had broken forth, leading her to commit the act she had done. Daisy had once left her luggage at the cloak- room of , Paddington Station and the happy :hought now occurred to her to go there and deposit her bag whilst she looked for lodgings; the fact that she only had twentf-three shil- lings or so in her purse did not alarm her; she felt sure of being able to obtain work of some sort—" Anything," she murmured to, herself; "even scrubbing floors." In her innocence and want of worldly knowledge she did not know that even to scrub floors training is re- quired, and that training must be backed by recommendations as to character, and that even with these the demand for work in Lon- don or anywhere else is always, and always will be, in excess of the supply. She took an omnibus to Paddington, giving the boy a shilling for his trouble in carrying the bag. At Paddington Station she left the bag at the cloak-room and obtained a ticket for it which she put in her purse. Then she started off to find a room. She tried ,Eastbourne Terrace. She had the most vague idea of what the price of a room by the week ought to be; she had never had to do for herself before or to consider money in any way, but she knew this, that the whole of her capital being about twenty-three shillings she could not possibly afford more than five, or at the most six, shillings a week for her lodging unless she was to starve.

The Eastbourne Terrace landlady wanted, however, ten shillings for a most gloomy and depressing-looking apartment upholstered in red and with a copper-plate engraving of the Day of Judgment over the fireplace. -- "Thanks," said Daisy, "I'll think about it and call again." "Boot cleaning and candles are extra," said the landlady, rubbing the knuckles of her right hand in the palm of her left as if she were polishing an imaginary boot and looking at Daisy like a furtive ogre, taking in the value of her hat and her clothes in general with the astuteness of a Jew. " Thanks," said Daisy, "I'll call again. That is to say, if I decide, you know." "You wouldn't like to leave your name and address in the country, would you ? " asked the landlady, following her prospective lodger to the hall door.

"No, thanks," said Daisy ; " that is to say, not just now. I don't come from the country. Good-day." _ _

She escaped from the house feeling, as the fly might have felt, had she made her escape from the spider's parlour, and for some time she wandered about not knowing the neigh- bourhood, and at last, after several more en- counters with impossible landladies, she found a house in a street off Westbourne Grove which at least was clean and where she ob- tained a top bedroom for the modest sum of six shillings a week inclusive.

Having given her cloak-room ticket to tlie landlady, who said her son would fetch the bag, Daisy departed, intimating that she would not be back till evening and that she would get her dinner out. She had several plans in her head for obtain- in.' work; one as impracticable as the other. A girl at school, mad on theatricals, had stated one day to an admiring circle that any girl with good looks and the ordinary amount of brains could make a fortune on the stage. She said the thing to do was to go to a dramatic agent. These words carelessly dropped had taken root in Daisy's mind and were remem- bered now. She took an omnibus to Bond Street and went to the music shop with which she had always dehlt. They gave her the names of two dramatic agents, one in the Strand, the other in Southampton Street. She determined to go to the one in the Strand first.

Mr. Oppenheim's office (Oppenheim was the dramatic agent's name) was situated ,up a deep and dark and dusty stairway.

Mr. Oppenheim's waiting-room gave upon the Strand. It was comfortably furnished, .one might say gorgeously furnished, and there were a good many people in it—waiting. Men, some stout, prosperous looking, clean shaved, some not so prosperous looking, some dis- til:re-fly shabby, but all clean shaved. There were girls, one or two well dressed, that is to say, really well dressed, turning over the pages of the " Era" or "The Stage" ; others not so well dressed with faded faces blighted by that magician. who either destroys or im- mortalises—The Stage.

In an inner room sat Mr. Oppenheim pre- sumably seeing clients, for every 'now and then the door would open, a client would come out, and Mr. Oppenheim, a fat, dark, little man with a hooked nose and a pen behind his ear, would stand at the open doorway and survey the host before him as a butcher might survey sheep in a fold. Some he spoke to in the open doorway without giving them the honour of an interview in the sanctum; some he smiled upon and led by the arm (if they were men) into the inner room, closing the door as carefully as though they were treasures that might escape.

Daisy sat watching all this, interested and amused at first, then depressed. There was something in the atmosphere of the place that depressed her, or was it in the clients of Mr. Oppenheim. With a woman's-, instinct she knew that each one of them had surveyed her and summed her up; that the woirien- were ready to pick her to pieces and the men to be insolently familiar if she gave them a chance. She sat waiting her turn and had fallen into a sort of day dream in which the voices of the people around her had lost individuality and . were as far from a resemblance to human speech as the murmur of a cataract when she was brought to the reality of things by a voice addressing herd "Have you an appointment with me ? " She looked up; there were only a few people left, and Mr. Oppenheim was standing before her. and. addressing her, _

"Oh," she said with a start, "I beg your pardon. I came here—that is to say, I wish to see Mr. Oppenheim." "I am Mr. Oppenheim," said that gentle, mane " Come in, please."

He led the way into the inner office whis!s was furnished much like any other office in the City of London, except perhaps with regard to the walls, which were simply covered with photographs photographs of individual actors or actresses known to, or clients of, Mr. Oppenheim, and photographs of touring com- panies. Here and there was a play bill." " Tait a seat, plea-se," said Mr. Oppenhelin in a bu 'ness-like voice, seating himself at his desk and motioning Daisy to take the chair

that stood by " Wsat can Ido for you ? " " I called, said Daisy, looking at the little man and unable to make up her mind whether she liked him or not, "to see if you could give me some advice, I want to go on the stage. Mr. Oppenheim over looked his fair client She made the most pleasing and innocent picture possible in her fawn felt hat and blue serge tailor-made gown: j . You ask my advice about going on the ri' tage ? " said Mr. Oppenheim. "Yes," said Daisy. "Well," said Mr. Oppenheim, "my advice is—don't."

Daisy flushed, but the little man put up his hand as if to say, "Wait a moment." "It seems cruel I know," said he, "to say don't to a person who is burning to distin- guish themselves in art, still I say it, having said it I proceed to business. Have you any money?" " No."

"Have you any acquaintance with stage • matters ? " "You have never acted ? " "Oh, yes, in private theatricals—at school." " Quite so." said Air. Oppenheim, drawing a little picture on the blotting pad before him, then looking up; "you can't spend money on dresses, I suppose, as you have none." " I'm afraid not. No, I can't, it's better to be frank. I have no money to spend on any- thing." - "How long have you left school ? " Mr. Oppenheim at the new, question began a new picture on the blotting pad. "A year and eight months." "Do you live with your—excuse me these questions—your mother ? " " No."

Mr. lOppenheinr rose up, went to the win- dow, looked out, jingled the keys in his pocket and then spoke. "You have neither ;money nor knowledge of dramatic art. Dear me, young ladies come tome and.say, Mr. Oppenheim, I want to go on the stage, as if I had only to open a door and let them on. Gooy-I gracious, the stage, my dear child, is a profession far more difficult than law or medicine and far worse paid. You. must work, work,_ work and then in the end

perhaps fail. Girls think that you have only to wear pretty dresses and you're sure ol -success. True, if you can spend five thousand on dresses and make yourself otherwise notorious people will come to look at your dresses,but what position are you in then? The position of a manequin at Pa pin's or Red- fern's, a thing to exhibit clothes upon, not an actress. My dear young lady, I am afraid I can do nothing for you, but here is the address of a lady who gives instruction in the art of acting; if you have any dramatic genius she will develop it. Her fee is twenty guineas for a course, I believe, and now listen to me. Don't go to another agent; a good agent can do nothing for you at present, and the bad' agents are far worse than thieves." He showed her out. In Southampton Street it took her a little while to recover from her first attempt at ob- taining employment. With all her timidity and all her innocence, however, she had a bold heart and was not to be daunted. She made for Messrs. Short and Sharp, the other dramatic agents, whose office was situated in the Strand. In the excitement of her situation she had almost forgotten Arthur. The wound that her love had received was still fresh and still bleeding, but the new life which surrounded her distracted her attention—even as she walked down the Strand the flaring yellow newspaper placards were proclaiming to the world;

GREAT BANK ROBBERY. CLERIC AND LADY IMPLICATED. FLIGHT OF THE WOMAN.

Even if she had read it she would never have guessed that the clerk was Arthur Esmond, and that the woman referred to was herself, Daisy Graham*. . .

' Al Messrs. Short and Sklarp's they offered to put her name on their books for the modest sum ,of a guinea. The gentleman who offered to do this generous act was oily haired and glib of tongue. Daisy had been undecided as to "'nether she liked or disliked little Mr. Oppenheim; she was not in the least undecided about Mr. Short. She didn't like him at all. She left without parting with her money, fortunately, and took her way along the !Strand towards Somerset House. She passed Somerset House and walked half way across Waterloo Bridge. Then she stopped and gazed at the muddy, river flowing in sinister and snaky eddies past warehouses, barges, buildings, :and the misery' and wealth of Lon- don to be lost for ever in the sea. A [great depression had came upon her, spirits, everything seemed so hopeless and London was so gigantic, so merciless, so deaf. She made several more attempts that after- noon to obtain employment without success ; people seemed to look upon her with suspicion ; she was too well dressed, in fact, to be looking for a situation.

At dusk, weary and footsore, she was stand- ing by the Serpentine. She had no purpose of suicide in her mind, but she was in that con- dition of despair with everything that the slightest tip of the balance would have made her rush into the water and end at once her misery and her life. The troubles of the day had been bad enough; the dark and dreadful future was bad enough. Then suddenly she recalled Arthur's words at their last parting, his protestations of love:

"My God," she cried, "have pity on me," and drawing a deer long breath she turned, wearied of life, wearied of all things, towards the chill and darkening water.

CHAPTER XII.

WE left Arthur Esmond under the very mild surveillance of Mr. Struthers. Struthers, who was man of the world above everything, and who had gained a wide and ;varied experience Of men and things, first as cabin boy on a ship, then as purser, then as member of the Chicago police, and lastly as bank porter, Struthers was so well convinced in his own mind of the innocence of Arthur that he did not hesitate to leave him to his own devices whilst he himself took tea down- stairs in Mrs. Brown's parlour, That night Arthur did not go to bed. The horrible misfortune that had happened to him bad thrown him completely off his balance. The loss of the money was bad enough, one might have imagined that nothing could be worse, yet to him worse than the loss of the money was the fact that Daisy Graham's name was dragged into the affair; viorse still was the fact of the unfortunate paragraph in her letter. If she should be suspected,

As he paced the floor of his rcom he prayed fervently to God that Daisy would do the thing which she had not done—tell the exact meaning of the paragraph to Mr. Rowlands, and so by backing up his explanation prove her own innocence and his.

At eight o'clock the next morning he set off for the bank with Struthers, just as up in Hampstead Daisy was making for Padding- ton Station, a boy beside her carrying a glad- stone bag. They arrived at the bank before anyone else and Struthers went off to don his livery, whilst Arthur Esmond sat turning over the leaves of three morning papers he had bought on the way. In none of them was any reference made to the great robbery. He drew a sigh of relief. The respite was only temporary. The after- noon papers would be full of the news no doubt, bnt still it was a respite. He dreaded the questions of his brother clerks, although he was as innocent as the babe unborn. The

story he had to tell was on calm' reviewai absurd on the face of it,

He had accompanied an old gentleman in the street and their lost consciousness. He had also lost over forty thousand pounds worth of valuables. In his desk had been found a letter from a lady containing a highly suspicious paragraph and telling him in fact to beware of the police. Could anything be worse. The first of the clerks to arrive was a gentle- man named Higgins, one of those depressing people who are always in high spirits. He asked Arthur whether he was going into the newspaper trade, and heedless of the latter's moody and irritable! manner described a party he had been at the pight before with a full and glowing account of the girl he had lanced with, an account which was com- pleiely thrown away upon Arthur. Mr. Rowlands always entered the bank by a side entrance, and he was always punctual to the moment.

Arthur pretending to be busy with a column of accounts on a piece of paper felt the place around him fillipg with life as clerk after clerk entered rapidly on the heels of one another and took their. places. In a moment the building was humming; a deep sonorous sound filled the place from all the floors above and below; punctuated by the sound of swing doors flying open or closing and the musical clink of gal upon copper as the cashiers began dealing with the first cheques, for at a great London bank there is always a customer, or two who wants a cheque cashed immediately the door is opened. • The clock pointed to ten minutes past, the hour of Mr. Rowlands' arrival. Arthur from where he sat could see the Bank parlour door and its shiny brass handle, he saw the door open and Struthers go in and conic out again with an armful of papers just as he had done every morning since Arthur had joined the bank. He saw the chief cashier go in just as he went in every morning and come out just the same with no glance at him, Arthur, Cr look of suspicion, or look of surprise. There was something sinister in this ap- parent indifference to the tragedy of yester day. The great money machine called the London and Lancashire Bank seemed not in the least put out because a fly had got en- tangled amidst its machinery, a fly which the next revolution of its wheels was going to crush. The next revolution came.

The bank parlour door opened and Stru- thers came out. He made his way zig-zag- ging between desks towards Arthur. Struthers face was the face now of the cross tempered wall-ey4ed bank porter. He spoke to Arthur just, as he always spoke when conveying a message to a Clerk from the manager, that is frigidly, yet not in the ltast disrespectfully.

" Mr. Esmond, Mr. Rowlands wishes to speak to you in the parlour." "Thanks,' said Arthur, swallowing hastily, rising from. his chair, shutting the lid of his desk with a bang, and following Struthers. Mr. Rowlands was not alone ; two gentle- men were seated near, him. The bank manager himself was seated -just where he always sat att the ,big mahogany desk table. The great clock waS ticking leisurely, just as it always ticked, not in the least flurried by the fact that the London and Lancashire money machine was about to extinguish a career and pass on to `the turning over of money heedless of the pity of it all. The light of day came through the high windows to light up the scene, falling upon Mr. Rowlands' bard head, the papers before him, the gentlemen beside him, and the pale strained face of Arthur Esmond as he stood before this council ,ol three.

On the right of- Mr. Rowlands sat Lord de Morley, on the left Mr. Morgan, of the Criminal Investigation Department. ' This is Mr. , Esmond," said Mr. Rowlands, looking t Arthur and speaking to the two gentlemen on either side of him. .

- Arthur bowed. It was a• trying, position, the light full in his eyes and his three inquisi- tors before him, yet he met it with a certain quiet dignity which had its effect upon one at least of his hearers.

Lord de Morley began the interrogation, speaking in a quick irritable voice and jerky manner.

" Well, then—you are the gentleman to whom frny property was entrusted— what have you to say—what account have you to give of yourself? '

Arthur went through the whole experience, describing 'everything in the minutest. detail. " Is that all,? " " Yes," said Arthur'. " Well, •I know all that. Well, then, tell me this." He looked straight in Arthur's eyes and so piercingly that surely if there had been any guilt in the young man's mind he would have seen it.

"Tell me this—have you any private idea of who robbed you? " "None."

" You—excuse the brutality of the question —are not a thief yourself or in collusion with thievek? " • 'No:' not."

" No sir," cried Arthur indignantly, " I arn

" I believe you," said his lordship, twirling his moustache and sinking back in his chair; he seemed to fall into a reverie. Then Mr. Rowlands seeming not te.. heed what Lord de Morley said turned to the man on his left.

"'You have heard-the story, • Mr. Estnond has told us; this letter," drawing Daisy Graham's letter from his pocket, " I found in his desk; this paragraph I have marked, read it."

" You have no right to show every'one that letter, sir," cried Arthur, making a step as if to tear the document from the detective's hand. "You found it by chance in my desk. I let you read it because otherwise you said you would ttand it over to the police for them to read; owing to an unfortunate sentence in it you suspected a lady, and I let you keep it that you might show it to her and so prove her and my innocence." "I showed it to her," said Mr. Rowlands calmly, "and it has proved, if anything, her guilt—and yours." Arthur stepped back as if from before a blow.

"Her guilt I " he cried. "You told me a cock and bull story," said Mr. Rowlands, "invented, I feel certain, on the Spur of the moment. She tells you to beware of being caught in that paragraph, to beware that the police don't see you. You said this referred to an appointment you had with her in the garden of her aunt's house, and the fact that to meet her you would have to scale a Wall. If the young lady had sub- stantiated this story I would have believed it, but when she absolutely refused to explain the meaning, though I told her it was a vital matter, I must disbelieve it, and in fact, to cut the matter short, I am bound, as a matter of course and in the interests of the bank, to give you in custody. I -don't think," said Mr. Rowlands judicially, and speaking apparently to the ink pot, ' that a magistrate on the evidence will convict or send the case to a jury, still it is my duty, and a very painful one to place the matter in the hands of the authorities, for them to deal with." A knock at the door. Cotie in," cried Mr. Rowlands, cutting or, his discourse and looking up with a shade of irritability on his face: - _•

"Then show her in at once." Struthers vanished. " I expect the lady who has just arrived," said Mr. Rowlands," will be able to shed some new light .ion the case; it is, in fact, Miss Princeps;the aunt of the—" ‘` Miss ,Princeps," announced Struthers. The flight b. Daisy only two hours ago—as she came down to breakfast she had received the news—the fact that she had not been able to touch breakfast, the long journey from Hamp- stead had all conspired, to put Miss Princeps in Dne of the most ferocious tempers into which it had ever been her lot to fall. . _

" Ah, there you are," said she, speaking to Mr. Rowlands and totally disregarding every- one else.

Thispatch took place at Gorsey-lane on Saturday. Mr. Neild (manager for Messrs. Wright scored again for Rylands, and the game ended in a win for the Wiremen by 2 goals to nil.

The prospect of a through tram service between Manchester and Liverpool is said to be remote. Owing to a rumour as to the difference in gauge between, the Manchester and Salford and the south Lancashire systems a correspondent on Wednesday visited the point where the lines actually touch at Winton, the boundari of Eccles borough an Worsley. The workmen erect' ing the overhead equipment expect that the South Lanbashire line through Worsley will be open in a few weeks. Where the two systems' lines come in contact the guage shows a difference of an inch, and several measurements made by one of the gangers show that the South Lancashire Company's gauge is 4ft. Sin., and the Manchester and Salford gauge 4ft. Oiin. (4ft. 91in.)

BEAUTIFUL WARRINGTON SOCIETY. FIRST ANNUAL SHOW.

If proof were needed that the Beautiful Warrington movement is progressing, it was forthcoming on 'Saturday when the first annual exhibition of flowers, plants, window boxes, and hanging baskets was held in the Parr Hall. The large attendance demonstrated the great interest of the townspeople in the work. Especially pleasinftwas it to see the juveniles present in such strong force, for when their sympathies have 'been enlisted success must he within measurable distance. The show itself wae exceedingly creditable to an industrial town like Warrington, and the hall presented a very bright an picturesque appearance. Although the number of competing exhibits was not very large, the specimens presented showed that great care had been bestowed upon their cultivation. Then too it must be remembered that the movement is in its infancy, and no doubt when another year has passed more enthusiastic support will have been enlisted and keener competition engendered. There is no doubt whatever that the Society is making splendid headway in the junior section audit was gratifying to see such a fine display of plants and bouquets of wild flowers brought to the hall by the youngsters. Non-competing exhibits, too, provided a striking and attractive feature. There were some magnificent groups of plants and flowers, the centre piece-10 feet in diameter —being sent from the Perk. The Society was indebted to the following for non-competing exhibits :—The Mayor of Warrington' the Corporation of Warrington, Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall, Members of the Stockton Heath Garden Society, Mr. and Mrs. Long, Mr. and Mrs. Lyon Mrs. Stanton, Mr. Henry Roberts, Mr. Gordon Rofaerts, Mr. F. R. Roberts, Mr. Tandy, Mrs. Massey, Mr. Vera Davies, Mr. Leonard Davies, Dr. Markel, Mr. Greening, Mr. J. Fairhurst, Mr. T. J. Lee, Mr. E. Slater, Mr. Ward (section of a bee-hive), Mr. W. Powell. The work of judging was undertaken by Mr. J. Parkinson and Mr. Poulton Whose decisions appeared to give satisfaction. The arrangements had been admirably carried oueby the committee and officials, a special word of praise being due to Mr. J. Fairhurst, the energetic hon. secretary, and to Mr. A. Fawcett, secretary of the Show Committee. During the afternoon and evening Mr. T. J. Down's orchestra was in attendance and sub- mitted an excellent programme which gave the greatest pleasure. Mr. Arthur Down conducted.

===== 0002601_19060908_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OPENING CEREMONY.

The show was opened by Colonel R. Ireland Black- burne, C. 8., president of the society, who was accom Ponied by Mrs. Blackburne. Canon Morley Stevenson (chairman of the Executive Committee) presided and amongst others present were :—Mrs. Heywood Bright, Mr. and Mrs. Henry Greenall, the Rev. W. Stanton, Alderman Bennett and Mr. John Fairhurst (hon. secretary). Mr. FAIRHURST read the following report on the outside exhibits, including fixed window boxes and back and front gardens. The judge (Mr. W. H. Woodcock) wrote :—" Gentlemen,—ln handing in the list of suc- cessful competitors for the outside exhibits, we think it advisable to add the accompanying remarks. That much would be gained if the members in laying out their gardens would choose a simple design, more especially where space is limited ; walks curved rather than angular ; beds circular or oval, and the space for flowers as open as possible. In planting the flower borders, the object should be to obtain a succession of bloom, effective contrast and gradation of height. We consider that the society could do some of its best work by encouraging the residents in many of the streets to elver into a friendly rivalry to make each individual street as attractive as possible. Canvassing done by the Ward Committees would do much to give this movement a start. We noticed a very large number of front gardens at present quite neglected and weedgrown which by attention could be made veryattractive. dmost every street provides some examples of this. Considering the short existence of the society the efforts of the com- petitors were satisfactory." One street entered into competition (Wakefleld.street), and the judges (Messrs. Usan and W. H. Woodcock) awarded the prizes as pii..°,37—First prize. No. 53; second prize,No. 47;third Lateb7;,s, The ward prizes is ere awarded as follows :- 2 Peter Clll Ward—Small front garden: 1, H. Harmop ; L'awrence 'lei 3. H. Lawrence. Back garden : 1, H. garden-I,;A2.'RPeter Cliffe. Fairfield Ward—Small front 2• Yates;R 2, R. Lunt. Back garden: 1, James Anders; Hall Ward—s Lunt ;3, William Hopkins. Town mall front garden : 2, Austin Hankinson. Whitecross Ward_Window box : The only entry in this chug was more of a bay window display than a window box. We have therefore, awarded a special prize equal to a first prize to John Parkinson." The school children's competition report read "Some time ago the society was able with the very generous assistance from Sir Peter Walker, Bart.. and others to distribute young plants among the school children. The venture has proved a success. In the competition both successes and failures have been brought forward; there are, however, very few in- grown specimens. The causes of unsatisfactorY growth have been explained to the children, and are mostly due to lavish watering and want of drainage. The number of plants presented for competition was 1,196, out of the number distributed (2,500), which is considered satisfactory. We congratulate the society on its most noble work ; Mr. Longshaw, the secretary of the school children's competition, for his labours bestowed ; the masters and mistresses on the admirable arrangement made at the exhibitions, and kindly interest shown generally ; and last, but not least, the children themselves. Nothing gave us creater pleasure than to see the enthusiasm created amongst them. We believe that in this venture the society have hit upon their most ennobling acheme. It is eminently health- ful, as a hobby ought to be. It is a pursuit conducive in a very high degree to the cultivation of refined taste, and being in a double sense a sign of culture and it decorator of the Home Beautiful it has no equal." (Applause). Canon MoRLEy STEVENSON expressed the gratitude of the society to the kind friends who had lent exhibits (not for competition)which had added very much to the beauty of the show,to Mr. Down for having brought his excellent band to enliven the proceedings, and to msny other people for acts of kindness and service. The Society wanted to encourage the growth of plants in all the homes of the town and in all the schools. They wanted to make the home beautiful, and they honestly believed there was no-one in Warrington who could not grove a plant or plants in their own house and help to make that house more beautiful than it was now. He would ask the visitors particularly to look at the exhibits which had come from the houses of the people and from the schools of the borough, and he thought they would see that although the show had been got up at a some- what short notice, real efforts had been and were being made to beautify the home. With regard to the children's section, no-one had entered into the work of the Society more heartily than the teachers and the children in the schools. He was sure the visitors would agree that the plants grown by the children, 'which were on view in the corridor,were very creditable indeed to youthful beginners. (Applause.) Colonel IRELAND -BLACKBDRNE said heffully appre- ciated the honour which the Society had done him in asking him to open their first show, as his family bad been long connected with the town of Warrington. The Colonel proceeded to review the position of the Society, -Pointing out that it had now between 500 and 600 full Members and a juvenile section 2,500 strong. During the year two open spaces—one near Fairfield Hall and the other in Knutsford-road—had been planted with tress and shrubs. In addition 500 window boxes had been distributed and very much brightened the apnea r- ance of the streets. The present show was an evidence Of what could be accomplished in the growth of plant and flowers even in a commerical town like Warrington. The heartiest thanks of the Society were due to Canon Morley Stevenson and to Mr. John Fairhurst, the indefatigable hon. secretary—(applause)—as well as to Manv others who bad laboured most assiduously to make the kociety a success. Canon Stevenson had written a most interesting pamphlet on "How to make Warring. ton beautiful" and he (the speaker) commended it to the careful perusal of all who wished well to the move- ' 41ent. Colonel Ireland Blackburn°, said that among the many favourable circumstances which had encouraged them in the work of the society, one of the.most gratifying was to secure Colonel Ireland Blaokburne as the first president. The choice was most appropriate because the family had long been intimately associated with the town and one of his (Alderman Bennett's) earliest recollections—a recollection which he still cherished— was of the old avenue leading to Colonel Ireland Black- burne's ancestral home and of the obelisk at the end of it which commemorated the virtues of one of the Colonel's ancekors who was well-known as an eminent botanist. The member of the family commemorated by the obelisk formerly lived at the hall and was the second gentleman in Britain to successfully grow the pine- apple and the first to produce the cotton plant. A dress was actually made from the cotton grown there and a member of the family Intended to go to Court in it but was pre- vented by bereavement. He (Alderman Bennett) remem- bered in the days gone by seeing the late Colonel Black- burns riding to and fro among the green fields that former- ly existed near that hall. After that retrospective glance, If one might take a look into futurity, he hoped that as a result of the work of the Society they would once more have avenues as beautiful as the old Orford-avenue and green fields in the heart of the town. He welcomed the presence of Colonel and Mrs. Ireland Blackburne and trusted it would be the beginning of a much closer connection between them and the town. (Applause.) The Rev. W. STANTON seconded and said the changes affected by the society's work were already noticeable as one drove into the town. Of course there was always the temptation in a place like Warrington, where there was so much smoke and a great deal of discouragement to the growing of plants, to think that such efforts were of no use. English people, however, prided themselves upon not being beaten, and on succeeding in the face of difficulties, so they who were engaged in that good work must per- severe to make Warrington beautiful. (Applause.) The vote was heartily accorded, and Col. IRELAND BLAcasurtm responded. THE PRIZE LIST.

GIRLS AND BEAUTY FOODS.

Much of the " muddy," coarse, or other complexion blemishes are produced by the inordinate craze many girls have for acid flavourings and strong tasting sweets that directly produce impurities in the blood. The King's physician advises a more plentiful use of dried currants, which contain a very delicate and agreeable acid, and are nearly all nourishing grape sugar.

Warrington P.S.A.

The usual meeting of this society was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, on Sunday, Mr. J. W. Sherlock presided, and gave an address on "The importance of individual choice." Mr. john Gill pointed out how every man was responsible for help- ing forward the coming of God's kingdom on earth. Mr. Percy Denby rendered two solos in an efficient manner, and Mr. R. Nelson read the Scripture lesson. On the motion of Mr. Wynne Jones, seconded by Councillor Clegg, a vote of condolence was passed with the widow of the late Mr. Henry Taylor (police court missionary). It was largely through the efforts of Mr. Taylor that the Warrington P.S.A. was started nearly 14 years ago, and Mr. Taylor was himself associated with the P.S.A. for mans years.

"He's a cove with a lantern phiz, broken nose, and a cock-eye, and a dimon' as big as a nut on his finger." Dewhurst opened a secret drawer and took out a roll of bank-notes, which he handed over. "Now go!" he roared, for a moment losing control of himself, and White fled like a scared rabbit. Dewhurst mounted a bicycle and rode into thE town, where he sent off a cable announcing the seizure by the Government of twenty thousand ounces, and asking for instructions. War had not yet been declared, and there was just a chance that the message would get through. If it did get through hq could not expect a reply until next day, and in spite of the urgent advice of those friends who were among the last to leave the city, he decided to wait. The reply to his cable was delivered next morning, and he read at the same time an order in Dutch posted up in the post-office announcing that no more passenger trains would be despatched. The cable was short, and completed , the load of his troubles. It read: "Resignation ' 'accepted. Hand all books and documents over to Carl Muller." It was signed Maynard. I Dewhurst stood on the stoep Staring blankly over the Market-square, as the iron sank into his soul. He had not offered his resignation, he had not expected his dismissal, but what hurt him more than the cruelty of his discharge when he had remained at his post after others had fled, was the order to give up the books to Carl Muller, the man he disliked of all others, who had aspired to the! hand of Minnie Maynard. "Muller," he said, and as he depicted the triumph of his rival lea suddenly turned- white tc the lips for in a flash he identified "The Boss," described by White, with Muller. The mean rascal's description ci "The Boss" as a man with a broken nose' lantern face, and a cock eye had not, when related, carried with it any resemblance tc men of his set, but now the picture, though over- drawn, came home. Muller had a defect in one eye, his face was long and sallow, and his thin hooked nose was slightly crooked from a blow he had received in a brawl, and as he recalled many suspicions openly directed against the man, he had no doubt that Muller drew his wealth, the source of which had always been vaguely covered by the term "Commissions, from illicit traffic in gold !amalgam stolen from the mines, and of which it had been reckoned that over a million sterling had been lost to the companies. t' By the .Lord," said Dewhurst, "I'll run the rogue to earth," and, as he roused himself out of his stupor, he saw crossing the square a party of three mounted Boers escorting a mule waggon. j They were Bartman, and the two burghers who had ! visited the office the day before. He turned his back until they had passed, then mounted his horse and rode back to the mine. After a wary inspec- tion of the scattered premises, he secured the rope over the main shaft and descended. He went down well-groomed and well-dressed, but emerged after i a lengthy spell, with grime rubbed into his skin, dressed in an old suit of faded corduroys with rusty veldschoens and a big "smasher" hat, or wideawake, encircled by a strap. He carried a Mauser rifle and swung a bandolier over his shoulder. Removing the neat pig-skin saddle from his horse, he girthed on instead a wide Boer saddle with big saddle-bags. Sitting his horse like a sack of flour, and smoking a heavy, brass-mounted pipe, he made a wide turn and re-entered Johannesburg. A hard looking Boer, somewhat better dressed than his type, hailed him. It was Commandant Cronje, a pretty familiar figure in Johannesburg, and Dewhurst had need of all his grit to assume the passive, imperturbable look of a burgher. "Who are you? Where do you go? Where are you from ? " snapped out Cronje. "Good-day, Oorn," said Dewhurst, in Dutch, handing over his tabak "sack" or skin pouch of generous proportions. "I ride to join my Field Cornet."

"What Commando?" "The Kornati Commando. I was visiting my uncle Oud Jan Marais when I got the word, that is why I am so far off just now." "That's a good horse. I have seen it in town I think."

I"Ja, Ooni,- that is true. My own horse was poor 1 and I took this from a Rooinek. Well, I must be ! going, the Field-Cornet, is a quei (peppery) man. So long," and, touching the horse, he rode off, leaving the redoubtable commandant wondering where he had seen this off-hand young burgher before. He was challenged several times by burghers before he got out of the town, but the Boers were so elated at the prospect of war that they paid him little attention, and once on the veld he was for the time out of danger. , Dewhurst had spent a couple of years in the I north big game hunting, and his bush-lore now I served him well. Picking up the spoor of the ! mule waggon he followed it for two days, till he . struck the Middleburg Station on the line from I Pretoria. Here he told the same story he had I invented for Cronje's benefit, and, having ascer- tained that the three Boers had gone on by train to I Komati Poort, he demanded acconnuodation for I himself and-horse. I "Where's your money ? " asked the station- master, one of the rapacious gang of Hollanders I who prey on Britons and burghers alike. I"Pay! What! would you have a burgher pay • who is going forward to fight ? " ' "What is this noise ? " demanded an old Boer. "This skepzel," raged Dewhurst, "wishes to fill is pockets with Boer money while we fight." I "Hit him on the bead," said the old Boer, with • a glance of bitter dislike. "Right," he added as the Hollander sat down with his hand to his eye. I"What is your Commando, Neef ?" "The Komati Commando," said Dewhurst. . I"The Commandant—his name ? " ! "I know not whether it is Lucas Meyer, or de Smit." I "I am the Commandant." ' "My word!" said Dewhurst, as a cold chill ran down his backbone. "I am lucky to 'find my !Commandant. You will let me be your rider? I "My rider, and you know not the name of your Commandant!" said the old man sternly, but Dewhurst had been using his eyes, and saw the initial "G," so he answered, quickly: "It is Commandant Grobler, but I thought all along he would surely lead on the Natal side." ' 1 "What is your name ? " 1 "Piet Marais." "Y- ik likr

"Ja, Ooni," said Dewhurst, htunbly, and when the train went on he rode in the same compartment ' with the Commandant, who watched him with grave eyes. Fortunately, the old Boer was not talkative, and he had too much natural dignity to be curious, so that Dewhurst escaped a dreaded catechism. In the evening he was able to do some services for the Commandant, and in the morning he brought dowy coffee, but when at last they reached Bomati camp, the mock burgher found that he was not to get off unscathed. "I have watched you, Marais," said Grobler, lifting .a big finger. "First, you think too much. !Next, there is something on your mind. You sit • square on your seat, there are no marks on your thumb from tobacco cutting, and your teeth are white. You are tiveld burgher only in your dress. 1 How is. this ?" Dewhurst returned the old mans not unfriendly look, but for the first time he was at a loss for an answer. "So! You have no words. Well, Marais; if that is your name, for the sake of the blow you gave that greedy thieving Hollander, I will say no more, except this—be careful." "Thank you," was all Dewhurst coul a say, an the old Boer playfully tapped him on the head and seized his aria in an iron grasp. "So 1" he laughed," the arm is Boer! The laugh and the familiarity did Bewhurst good, for the burghers in camp, seeing him on such good terms with their Commandant, did not worry him with questions. "Marais, Marais," cried a bull voice. "Someone calls you," said the burgher; an Dewhurst reluctantly returned the hail only to find, to his disgust, that he was , ordered out on picket duty-. He saddled up, and presently moved out of camp with a small party up the mountain to a "nek" crossed by a waggon road. Here they dis- mounted, and sought shelter behind detached rocks, for the wind blew cold over the narrow neck. Tio add to their discomfort a drizzling rain fell. "Mij Got I" said one man. "I would like well to be over there in Gert Bey' s place, for all ;,t has so bad a name." "Where is the place?" "There, kerel, there. See that little fire, like the glow of a man's pipe," and he pointed to a tiny glimmer, barely visible, away on the left. "Why has it a bad name?" "Well, they say a man was killed there, and that his spook cries in the krantz at the back of the house. Is that not so, Stefan ? " "Ja, Allematig ; the thing cries like a wolf so , that the blood in you turns to water. I have heard it." ' "Tell us." Stefan lit his pipe, and entered into the harrow- ing particulars, while the burghers clustered round him. Dewhurst slipped gradually into the rear, and as the interest increased, he edged off to where the horses were sheltered. Covered by the noise made by the wind, he led his horse off till it was safe for him to mount, then worked towards that little dim, flickering light ahead. The country was rough, and after groping about for a couple of hours he gave the horse a free head with success; for the intelligent animal, getting a streak of scent in the air, presently hit off a footpath which brought them out by a cattle kraal. Rounding this, Dewhurst saw a house a few yards distant, and dismounted to investigate. The wet did him a good turn in keeping the dogs indoors, and he found his way to the window undetwted. The picture disclosed within the little plain room, lit up by a couple of candles, filled him with a stern satisfaction that repaid him for the subter- fuges and humiliations of the past few days. At a small table sat four men, Bartman, the two burghers, and another, the dealer in stolen gold. Was this the man? Was this Carl Muller, the man who aspired to the hand of Miss Maynard, the plausible schemer who had ingratiated himself in the good opinion of Mr. Maynard—the fashionable lounger of Johannesburg? Was this "The Boss," the clever rogue who had, with his confederates, bled the mining Companies to the tune of a million? Dewhurst stared long and fixedly before he could recognise in the face or appearance of the fourth man the slightest resemblance to the fashionable Carl Muller, and then it was a gesture that gave him a clue. Muller had a way of spread- ing his hand from thumb to little finger when he was interested, and zitis man was doing the same. Otherwise he was a Boer like the other three, in • dress, in speech, and in a long, black beard, evidently worn in disguise. Muller sat at the top of the table, with the door on his left, and the other three facing him, while, as if by accident, he had laid his revolver before him. On the floor was e dull, yellow heap of bar gold, with one honeycombed mass taken from the retort, and on the table were many piles of sovereigns over which there was proceeding evidently a heated argument. They spoke so loudly that Dewhurst could hear every word. "You prornisea us good value," said Bartman. "Two pounds per ounce' was the price, and a good price." "Yes, good for you, but not for us." "If you want more take it to your Mint. Perhaps they will give you four pounds an ounce, perhaps they will give you nothing." "The Mint is the same as the Government." "All right. Accept two pounds from me or take It , to the Government and see what they have to say." "But the Government will give us nothing," said Van Noordt.

• "And I offer you twenty-five thousand pounds." "It should be more than that," said Bartman. "The weight is twenty thousand ounces." "Yes, my friend, but not all gold. There's my offer, and there's my money; take it or leave it." Van Isloo.rdt stood. up and began slowly counting

_ the little heaps. There were twenty-four heaps. Then he took one heap up and laboriously counted it out, knd announced the amount as one hundred pounds. "There are twenty-four heaps of one hundred," he said. "Yes," observed Muller, quietly, "twenty-four thousand pounds. Put down one hundred—so. That is one heap, now set twenty-four before the one hundred, that is twenty-four thousand pounds, the one hundred extra I throw:in for friendship." "But it seems to me," said Bartman, "there is something wrong. There are twenty-four heaps of one hundred. Is that not so? Now if I had twenty-four cattle kraals, with one hundred cows in each how, many would I have?" "We all know that Field-Cornets pick up cattle that don't belong to them," said Van Noordt. "Now, burghers all, don't quarrel. I'll tell you what I'll do. Accept the offer now and I'll throw in another fifty pounds for your wives." "Good," said Van Noordt, who was getting sleepy and wanted to finish off the business. So the three men took out their leather pouches, swept in their share of the gold, and rose to go. Dewhurst slipped back behind a stone wall facing the house and saw the burghers come out into the rain. "Better stay," said Muller. _ "We must get back to camp," growled Bartman. u Good-night. ' They stamped away, and Muller stood at the door for some time, looking after them before he returned to the room where the gold was stored. As Dewhurst rose he heard the burghers approaching, and saw that they had been to fetch their arms. As they entered the house Muller secured his revolver. "Back again ? " he said, giving them a keen look. "Changed your minds after all ? " "Carl Muller, listen to this," said Bartman. "I am here as a Field-Cornet to commandeer this gold for the Government." "But you have just sold it to me," said Muller, with an uneasy smile. "I sold it to you as a burgher. I seize it as an officer of the Government, by order of the President." "Do you really mean it ? "Put down .that pistol." "A good joke," said Muller, with a laugh. "Certainly," and he held the butt of the revolver to Barthian. As the Field-Cornet reached out fox it Muller disabled him with a kick and shot at Rey, but Van Noordt firing from his hip, ended the career of "The Bbss." When Bartman had recovered and Rey's wound was doctored, the three burghers carried the gold -out to the cart where Dewhurst followed them. They made several journeys, then returned to efface all evidence of the crime that might tell against them; and while they were gone Dewhurst drew the cart to where his horse was tied up, and, unslinging his Mauser, prepared to fire. He found, however, other aid. As the burghers were bearing off the body of the dead man a jackal howled from the krantz above, and Dewhurst heard their frightened exclamations concerning the spook. Re recalled an incident connected with Governor Van Oordt, and in deep, menacing tones called Out: "Van Noordt, Van Noordt, jij es dood (you are dead) t " Dropping the body the burghers ran for the cart, not finding it where they had left it, thinking aossiblv that the horses had bolted in terror. they made off Into the clam aown the road. As they did so Dewhurst, tying his horse behind the cart, drove off in the opposite direction, and in the early , morning entered a wild ravine where he hid the , treasure. Then, continuing for some distance, he released the horses from the cart, turned them free, set it rolling over a precipice, and himself escaped ' over the mountains, finally reaching Delagoa Bay. There he wired that he had recaptured the gold and was taken again into favour. Vila Exp.]

COMMAND EFRED GOLD.

By ERNEST GLANVILLE,

Author of "The Despatch Rider," "The Rloof Bride," The Fossicker," "Tales from the Veld," dm.

John Dewhurst WU mining manager of the famous Witpoortje gold mine of Witwatersrand, which pays fifty per cent. every year in dividends, and had an output running isto twenty thousand ounces per month.

He had a princely salary, a fine house in Jeppe's Town on the outskirts of Johannesburg, and A future that was brightened by the promise of an alliance with sreet Minnie Maynard, the only daughter of Mr. Charles Maynard, the Chairman of the Witpoortje Gold Mining Company. • •

Yet he stood at his office door looking with an air of utter dejection at the silent works which stretched before him in an untidy jumble of iron sheds, connected by lines of tramways, and flanked by huge mounds of tailings from the stamp-mills, and of white mud heaps from the cyanide vats.

The mine had closed down; the drives and cross- cuts no longer echoed to the deep rumbling made by the rock drills which were wont to batter for hours of the busy day at the hard face of the blue " banket," as the conglomerate is called; but what oppressed him more was the unnatural silence of the hundred stamp-mills which since their erection had run almost night and day, Sundays and week days, holidays and feast'-days, battering the hard rock into pulp with their sledge-hammer blows, and setting up a noise that was like the roar of a heavy surf. He walked into the deserted mill-house and looked at the long row of gleaming rods, each with its foot of hard steel, at the still rockers, and the long rows of frue-vanners, which, when set going by the great shaft, would quiver and shake all ways at once, agitating the fine, flour-like particles of gold till out of weariness they would sink into the grooves to be afterwards gathered and retorted. 'Already it seemed to him the vast and valuable machinery had visibly deteriorated, although the works had only been silent for two days, and he turned away with a sigh to walk among the great grey cyanide vats, up to the tr..:l tower above the main shaft, and back to his office.

In one week his hopes had been dashed to the ground. People had been stampeding from Johannesburg for quite two months previously under the fear of the outbreak of war; but he had laughed at their fears and had made light of the warnings of the Board of Directors. He had even gene so far as to disregard the positive orders of Mr. Maynard, the Chairman, to send away the gold already secured without waiting for the end of the month. Now war was at hand, and caught him unprepared. His fine house, with its costly new furniture got out at great expense, and lovingly arranged by him with thoughts of the young . mis- tress, was at the mercy of the swarms of white rascals and drunken natives who infested the place; but beyond all danger to his own property was the bit- ter thought that \his blind optimism had risked the property of the company. In bars and in the retorts were fifty thousand pounds' worth of gold, and he could not, for the life of him, tell how he was to clear it.

That question was soon settled for him. Three men, armed and mounted, suddenly reined up at his door. They were typical burghers, big, powerful men, roughly dressed, with shaggy beards, and faces tanned by exposure and the smoke from camp fires.

"You are the manager?" said the leader in English.

"Yes."

"You have gold here?" "There is, I hope, plenty of gold in the rock underneath."

"Take care, Englander. If you value your skin, answer quick and straight." Dewhurst flushed„up, and his square jaw took on a look of grim obstinacy. "Who are you? What authority have you to ask such a question ? "

The Boer drew out a document. "Read," he said, and thrust it into Dewhurat's hands. It was an order, signed by President Kruger himself, empowering Field Cornet Bartman to commandeer all gold on the premises of the Witpoortje Mine.

"Commandeer What is commandeer ? " "To take what is wanted for the Use of the Republic." "That is robbery, calhit what you like."

Field Cornet Baitman shifted his rifle till it pointed at the manager's breast. "I have authority to shoot all who resist."

"Resist I How can unarmed men resist ? If we had rifles we would know how to answer robbers." "Why do we talk?" said one of the other Boers in Dutch. "Shoot the rooinek."

"Hold still, Van Noordt. Hark, you Englander," said Bartman. "Use your tongue well, give us no trouble, and we will not harm you. Where is the gold?" "I won't tell you!" "So Is that your last word ? " "It is 1," said Dewhurst, savagely.

The . three Boers swung to the ground and advanced. Dewhurst struck out and knocked Van Noordt off his feet, but the two others overbore him, and when he was down Van Noordt kicked him heavily about the body. They took the keys of his safe from him, bound him, and left him battered and bleeding, while they opened the strong- room. Then they went to the door and shouted. A man came up, in whom Dewhurst recognised an old hand, discharged for stealing amalgam, a man named White, who went off to where the retorts were, with the Field Cornet. They were some time away, and when they returned they sat in the office over a bottle of whisky they found there, and talked together in Dutch. They were in the highebt spirits at the success of their raid, in greater spiritb than the mission seemed to warrant ;and Dewhurst, who understood enough of the "taal " to follow their conversation, caught an expression which awakened him out of his stupor of despair.,

"We will meet him at Komati," said Bartman, "and he will pay us two pounds the ounce." "In gold?" "Ja, in gold; and when he has paid us the price we will • commandeer' the bar gold which we have already sold him, so the Government will benefit, and the Outlanders will pay." ' "Allematig, Bartman, you are a slim (cunning) karel."

"It is a good plan this, oh?" "Ta, ja. We get our share, Government gets its own, and the Verdoinfle skepzel pays. He would give us two pounds an ounce, the rascal, when the real price is three pounds fifteen shillings. It is well that he should lose all." The burghers laughed, smacking their thighs, and presently left, taking the bullion away in a cart, and leaving Dewhurst on the floor bound. What a scheme was this he had heard I The commandeered gold was to be sold to some man at Komati, probably a rascally trafficker in stolen 'amalgam, and when the burghers had pocketed the receipts, some forty thousand pounds, they meant to recover the bullion from the paymaster and restore it to the Government, themselves keeping what they had been paid.

"Well, you dog, how do you like it?" White, the discharged servant, stood at the door. "I don't like it," said Dewhurst, quietly. "Oh, you don't, you swab. You did the grand over them as earns their living by the sweat of their brow, now look' at yourself. You're there, and I'm here:" White prowled about the room and came back growling at the greed of "those blooming Dutchies."

"I say you, what will you give me if I tell you where those Boers have gone to ? "

"Nothing."

"Perhaps you think you know. All right, smarty. Stay where you are." He slouched out and remained away for an hour, when he poked his head in. "Enjoying yourself ? "he asked. "Now, look hers, Dewliurst. You can't do any good lying there. What will you give me if I set you loose? Make it a hundred. Come, now, Dewhurst, don't be hard on a poor chap now. Make it a hundred." "I'll make it a hundred," said Dewhurst ; and White cut the rope. The manager staggered to his feet, washed the blood from his face, and carefully removed every speck of dust frolu his clothing. 'Going to a wedding, or may L,s a supper-party, eb ?",sneered White. "Loot nere, my man. want you to tell me the acme of the thief who buys stolen amalgam." "You're a cool one, and m»uistake. D'ye think I'd split on a pal, suppose, that is, 1 knew hia name "

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS, noy CURED _-- Aopointment of a Resident Medical U Officer. OF RINGIORM

FAREWELL VISIT OF MR. JENNER FUST. A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday under the presi- dency of Mr. J. Bißinge. There were also present : Mrs. Clay, Mrs. Wilkinson, Miss Broadbent, the Rev. T. R. Matthews, Messrs. W. Hickman, A. J. Jolley, G. Thompson, T. H. Ashcroft, R. Bolton, S. Davies, G. Mather, J. Cook, W. C. Brooks, J. W. Knowles, M. W. Seton Houghton, J. Payton, T. Ritchie, J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), and A. Wright (superintendent of Padgate schools): .

Mr. :Termer Fust, the retiring Local Government Board Inspector for the North-Western Poor Law Dis- trict, attended to bid farewell to the Board.

_ THE COTTAGE HOMES. The Cottage Homes Committee recommended that a gratuity of £2 be allowed to Miss Bennett in recognition of her special servioes during the recent occurrence of scabies in the homes. It was also decided that the tender of Mr. Critchley for the supply of 250 yards of cloth at 4s. id. per yard be accepted. A report on recognised homes for Roman Catholic children was submitted by the Chairman (Mr. Mather) and Mrs. Clay. After conversa- tion on the matter, it was agreed to further consider the subject on the Ist October, the Chairman promising to obtain some additional information.

Mr. MATHER moved the adoption of the minutes and said that owing to one of the officials leaving, Miss Bennett had to do double duty. With regard to homes for Roman Catholic children, it had been stated that there were vacancies in the Culcheth homes and the com- mittee thought it would be as well to make inquiries there. MTS. CLAY seconded.

Mr. AsHcRoFT believed that it was a waste of time for the deputation to visit any homes as there was plenty of room at Padgate. The only fault was that children who bad no right to be in the homes were sometimes kept there. The minutes were approved. 111/IVir AUDITOR. • .'The CLERK notified the appointment of Mr. Ernest William Hicks as district auditor fn.-place of Mr. Jordison who has been transferred to the South East Lancashire district. Mr. Hicks would be about the fifteenth auditor he (Mr. Sutton) had met during his term of office.

INCREASE OF SALARY. Mr. Frank H. ACKLAND wrote applying for an increase of his salary as relieving officer of No. 3 district. It was stated that Mr. Aukiand's present salary was £125 but certain fees brought it to £133. Mr. KNOWLES thought that as Mr. Aukland had served them well for fourteen years he was entitled to a salary of £l5O and he moved that that amount be paid to him. Mr. JOLLEY seconded and a letter from Mr. Brooks (chairman of the No. 3 Relief district) strongly recom- mending an increase and highly enlogising Mr. Aukland's work having bean read, the advance to £l5O was unanimously agreed to. MR. JENNER Fusrs INSPECTION. "EVERYTHING IN EXCELLENT ORDER." Mr. JEER FEST said he inspected the Workhouse on the 29th August and found everything in excellent order. There had been a certain amount of pressure on the space at their disposal, but by utilizing some wards in the old Infirmary which lent themselves to the pur- pose tolerably well, they had been able to deal with it. He was glad that in utilizing those wards, the Board had not forgotten the need of escape in case of fire and bad provided very satisfactorily for that. He had on several occasions brought before them what seemed to him to be the need of further means of esca e in other parts of the Workhouse. They had not quite taken the same view and he had no wish to keep hammering on at the same subjeot,but it was his duty to remind them that there were wards from which no second really practicable exit existed and that the responsibility for this must rest upon their shoulders and not upon his. He never went through the Warrington Workhouse without find- ing that improvements had taken place in various directions and his present visit afforded 'so exceptionto that general rule. Mr. Jenner Fust• expressed his approval of the re-painting of the house, the arrange- ments for the provision of a padded room, and the improvements made in the laundry. The provision for boys who were at the Workhouse before they went to Padgate left something to be desired, because there was no means of separating them satisfactorily from the other inmates. He was sure the Guardians would agree that it was extremely undesirable for boys to be mixed up with grown-up men even if it were only for a few days. He also found some girls being retained at the House on account of the want of room at Padgate. If they could keep down the numbers at Padgate, well and good ; but if they could not it might be worth theirwhile to consider whether it would not answer their purpose better to board out their children at cottage homes in preference to enlarging the buildings at Padgate. Mr. Jenner Fust bade fare- well to the Board and expressed his pleasure that the relations between the members and himself had been of such a harmonious character. After thanking the Guardians sincerely for their courtesy and consideration he voiced his acknowledgments to the able and experi- enced clerk of the Board,-who,if he forgot anything,had probably forgotten more Poor Law than he (Mr. Jenner Post ever knew—(Laughter)—and to the other officers of the Union.

Mr. S. DAVIES, who was a member of the Board when Mr. Jenner-Fust was appointed, and Mr. W. Houghton, who had the privilege of first introducing Mr. Jenner Fust to the Board, both expressed regret at his departure and wished him a long and happy retirement. The CHAIRMAN moved :—" That this Board learns with regret of the retirement of H. Jenner-Fust, Esq., and desires to place on record its high appreciation of the services rendered by him as General Inspector of the Local Government Board, and wishes him happiness and health to enjoy the leisure ho has so honourably deserved, trusting also that the nation will not entirely lose the benefit of his wide knowledge and ripe experi- ence as an administrator of the Poor Law." Mr. KNOWLES seconded. The CLERK supported on behalf of the officials, and said he remembered the first gentleman who visited the Board in the same position as Mr. Jenner Fust. but not as inspector, as in those days they were known as Poor Law Commissioners. The resolution was heartily passed, and Mr. J ENNER- FUST responded. RESIDENT MEDICAL OFFICER APPOINTED. Out of six applications received for the appointment of resident medical officer, the Special Committee appointed to consider the subject had selected the fol- lowing four to appear before the Board :—Dr.Alexander Allan, Glenalmond, Perthshire, N. 8.; Dr. John William Dale, Bunslet, Leeds ; Dr. Thomas Ed. Flitcroft, Cowpen, R. 5.0., Northumberland; Dr. John Michael Murphy, Burnley-ruad, Accrington.--Dr. Allen did not attend but the other three applicants were present. Mr. ASHCROFT : Is it a fact that these three gentle- men were intervieved last night? The CHAIRMAN said•the Special Committee met the selected applicants on Tuesday evening as they thought such a course would assist the Board. Mr. Aslicnorr thought the committee were wrong in interviewing the candidates. They should have left that to the Board. The CHAIRMAN said the candidates were conducted over the Infirmary and the work was fully explained to them. After each applicant had been interviewed Mr. Knowles moved the appointment of Dr. Flitcroft and Mr. Jolley moved the appointment of Dr. Murphy. A vote resulted in the seclection of Dr. Flitcroft by El votes to 6.

The appointment was then unanimously confirmed. In thanking the Board Dr. FLITCROFT assured them that he would do his best for the institution and the inmates.
