WHEN KING EDWARD ENGLAND AND happens to be on the Con- GERMANY. tin ant the gossips generally

connect his visit with inter- national politics, but when, in addition to the KING, both the Prime Minister and the War Minister are near enough to be consulted, and consultations do, as a fact, take place, then every- ' body is certain that the relations between our- selves and foreign countries are the subject of discussion. Recently the KING was seen to whisper to Sir HENRY CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN On the promenade at Marienbad, and the conclusion was immediately arrived at that the conver- sation had some reference to the recent meeting between the KING and the KAISER. It never seems to have struck these wiseacres that if the KING did wish to discuss such a subject with his FIRST MINISTER the public promenade at Marien- bad was hardly the place he would have selected for the purpose. As a matter of fact, the conver- sation, according to Sir HENRY CAMPBELL BANNERMAN, who has been asked the question point blank, consisted in his MAJESTY ; merely inquiring after the health of Lady CAMPBELL- BANNERMAN.

Whether, however, the relations between the Governments of England and Germany have or have not been the subject of discussion between the King and his Ministers or between the King and the Kaiser, certain it is that there has been a rapprochement between the two Monarchs, and that must be welcomed, for, say what we will, cordial relations between Monarchs are an important factor in the preservation of peace between the peoples. And by this time we all know KING EDWARD well enough to know that he is a diplomatist of the very highest capacity, and that his great desire is the maintenance of peace. The meeting at Friedrichshof has been invested by Continental journals with an importance which s at once a tribute to the power of the KING and an earnest of their own desire for friendly international relations.

In this connection an article which is to appear in the September number of the "Deutsche Revue," and which bears some evidence of having been inspired, justifies the importance which has been attached to the moeting of uncle and nephew. We are told that the element of personal estrange- ment between them has been definitely removed, and that the relations of England and , Germany were discussed frankly and without constraint, as was also the general situation. It appears that, among other things, the cordiality displayed in London towards Ger- many during the recent visit of the German Editors has rather been resented in Paris, and that therp has been some suspicion as to the sinority of l'entente cordiale. Perhaps this was only natural, for where Germany is concerned France is jealous. The war of 1870-1 is not forgotten, and the greatest menace to European peace is the still remembered dictum of Gambetta after the war : " Revenge; never speak of it, but always think of it." There are people in France constitutionally unable to understand that ['England can quite consistently be on good terms with both France and Germany at the same time. The tact of the King has, we are told, overcome the suspicions of France, and now it appears that we are on the high road towards a rapprochement with Germany. According to the writer of the article in the "Deutsche Revue," the belief was general a year ago that the relations between this country and Germany were so strained that a cordial understanding would have to be preceded by a sharp encounter, but now all danger is past. The cordial relations have come as the result of Royal diplomacy, and there is now no obstacle to cordial co-operation between the two Governments. The French entente,the desire expressed by the Prime Minister the other day to extend it to Russia when her I Government becomes more stable, the Spanish j match, and the arrangement of good relations

,`..::i':','i.."..ltifir:.';','0.,;'1.2'ic,

with Germany seem to promise that period of peace for this country which will enable the Government to turn its attention to domestic matters and to institute a policy of retrenchment which will be a great relief to the British taxpayer and a stimulus to British industry.

A more conscientious and THE LATE JUDGE painstaking judge than the BOwEN RowLANDS. late Judge Bowen Row- lands, K.C. it would be impossible to find, and by no-one will his death be more sincerely regretted than by those who shared with him the trying work of the Warrington County Court. He was the soul of courtesy and amiability and no person, however humble, ex- perienced harsh treatment at his hands. Of any- thing that savoured of sharp practices, however, he was the uncompromising foe and he did not hesitate to denounce unsparingly anyone who, in his judg- ment, had been guilty of misrepresentation of facts. He had, too, a very keen sense of humour and his apt witticisms enlivened many an otherwise dull sitting. But the quality which stood forth pre-eminently in his public work was his patience which seemed to be almost inex- haustible. He was willing to hear a great mass of evidence—often with only a very slight bearing on the point at issue—if he thought it might be the means of elucidating a single additional fact which would help him to a just decision. His summings- up were models of lucidity and he was one of the most fluent and attractive speakers on the Bench. He was of an extremely warm-hearted disposition and the poor who through stress of circumstances were forced to appear before him invariably received the most considerate and sympathetic treatment.

W 3 had thought that the THE MAYOR'S principle had been Milli' ALIDITOR. ciently well established in Warrington that no member of the Town Council should derive pecuniary bene- fit from his membership of that body. The ques- tion has, however, once more come to the fore and the most interesting debate at Tuesday evening's Council meeting was as to whether a Councillor is, under certain special circumstances, entitled to receive monetary reward from the Council for services rendered. It has been the custom for years in Warrington—as well as in some other towns—for the Mayor's auditor who, according to law must be a member of the Council, to be paid an equal share with the two elective auditors of the £9O annually allowed for auditing the Borough accounts. An exception to the rule was forthcoming in the case of Alderman Fairclough who, however, only nominally filled the office and declined to accept any remuneration. His successor has discharged the duties to the letter, and has been quite as painstaking as either of the other gentlemen who have scrutinised the books. The point which arose on Tuesday was whether he was entitled to the same fee as his predecessors in office, and the Council by a large majority decided in favour of the payment, although there appeared to be a strong feeling that the matter was deserving of fuller 'considera- tion before any similar situation has to be faced. Legal opinion is somewhat divided, but the Municipal Corporations Act is explicit enough on the broad principle we have alluded to. It distinctly affirms that a Councillor holding any office or place of profit or having employment with or on behalf of the Council is liable to disqualification. Against this it is argued that the payment to the Mayor's auditor is made under the Public Health Act and is therefore perfectly in order but, as Councillor Lord pointed out, the Municipal Corporations Act, with its definite statement, is of later date and should therefore take precedence. An eminent authority on municipal work to whom the point has been referred, after consulting legal works holds that a member of the Council becomes disqualified if he receives from the Council any money for any con- tract or employment. Obviously the payment in question is for employment and to us the argument of a past Secretary of the Municipal Corporation's Association that 'the Mayor's auditor can a fee without being disqualified as his office the gift of the Mayor and not of the Coma(' decidedly weak. The Corporation and no. Mayor provides the money,and we take it that L!'", Mayor appoints merely in his capacity as C him Magistrate and head of the Corporation. Putupg aside the legal aspect, which awaits a definite pronouncement from the Judicial Bench, the question has a moral side which Is too important to be overlooked. The 83'SW:a of remuneration might easily lend itself to abuse and we are strongly of opinion that the safest course, and the one which conforms to the unmistakably expressed feeling of the ratepayers, is for Councillors to adhere strictly to the principle that no profit should accrue to them by virtue of their position as public representatives. We believe that in the case of the majority of municipalities whose practice has been ascertained the Mayor's auditor is unpaid, and so serious is the question at issue in Warrington that we learn from a letter from Mr. James Dodds in another column that steps will shortly be taken to test the legality of the payment.

Is it not time the Warring - THE DIGNITY OF ton Town Council showed a THE CouNciL, little more regard to dignity

_ in the conduct of its meet- ings? As the highest local authority, one would expect the Council to set an example in the observance of the ordinary rules of debate, but at Tuesday evening's meeting decorum was at times flung to the winds. Questions were discussed in a free and easy style which was no doubt very amusing to the public assembled at the back of the Chamber, but the Council should have some higher object in view than the entertainment of the ratepayers. Now and then members showed an inclination to lose their tempers but the climax was reached when Councillor Wright came to words with the Mayor. The Labour man was highly incensed at being ruled out of order and an unseemly wrangle ensued, the Coun- cillor exclaiming at the top of his voice that "if he was to be bullied, he could bully as hard as anyone." Such scenes are far from edify ing, to say the least, and tend to lower the Council in the estimation of the community. A more rigorous observance of the orders governing debate would certainly facilitate the business-like transaction of municipal work and this can only be secured by the co-operation of the whole Council.

The lot of the Warring- HALF-PENNY TRAM ton tram conductor hal FARES, not been a happy one of late, for the simple reason that he has frequently been called upon to decide the apparently innocent question: What is a workingman? His perplexity has been occasioned by the recent decision of ' the Town Council to issue workmen's ticket* during certain hours at Id. fare. Naturally enough there has been a rush of passengers anxious to be enrolled as workmen, and many who would probably be indignant if included in that classification on ordinary occasions, have not been above demanding -Id tickets. What was the poor conductor to do ? If a passenger claimed to be a son of toil, who was to question the accuracy of his declaration ? The Tramways Committee at last took pity on their servants and drew up a new rule to the effect that anybody using the cars before 8 a.m. was entitled to purchase a return . ticket at single fare—the return half to be avail- able within six days of date of issue (Sundays excluded). The champions of the workingman, however, were not satisfied. They held that person—only a worker, of course—who wished to travel one way should be at liberty to do so for 18; ' So strong was their opposition that the proposal of the committee was withdrawn. Seriously, sca think that the demands of labour are a little unreasonable and we hope the committee will adhere to its intention of giving a trial to the new system which appears to be fair to everyone. -- Dr. 0-

P.oothall League—an excellent form of organisa- tion which gives due merit its chance, and. in Which the. best team almost invariably wins, If the matter were a question of securing that the Conditions for all should be quite equal the thing Would be simple. But financial considerations Will probably prevent the big counties like York- shire and Surrey from joining in a scheme which light affect their earnings from "gate money" iery considerably. How much finance is wrapped lip with county cricket may be gathered from some figures relating to Yorkshire's earnings this Year. Fourteen home mate.hes brought in a total of £6,433, but no less than £3,082 of this was due to two matches—that against Lancashire at Bradford and that against Surrey at Sheffield. That the .League system would be better for minor counties like Somerset or Northampton there is no gainsaying, and one may go further and aver that it would be better in the interests of the game and of the public who love a fair fight and like to see .the "little one" get a good place. But even the influence of Dr. Grace -and Lord Harris will be sorely taxed to get the scheme through.

The Henry Taylor Fund. We would call the attention of our readers to the advertisement which appears in another column inviting subscriptions to a fund which will be invested foi• the beneit of the widow of the late Mr. Henry Taylor, who has been left in ill-health and practically destitute. As it is pointed out, the work done by the late Mr. Taylor during his fourteen years' residence in Warrington as Police Court Missionary, in connection with temperance work and almost every movement for the relief of distress, and especially for the good of women and children, is well known and deserves recognition. ~,,. Cricket and football, like everything earthly, 23:lay be abused or given too much importance," says he Rev. James Forbes, vicar of St. Cuthbert's, arwen, in his parish magazine, " but the devil has !Inuch less chance of the lad who plays cricket and , ootball than of the lad whose mind and body are interested in nothing but loafing,eigarette smoking,&c." * * * What impressed me as the most pleasing feature of Saturday's Beautiful Warrington Show was that the Children took the liveliest interest in it. The were present in strong force and sent a grand collection of Plants and wild flowers for competition. The Society nould not have done better than enlist the help of the rising generation and we believe that in the .. ouvenile section the movement will have its greatest source of strength.

* * The ideal of a Beautiful Warrington is slowly becom- ing nearer realization. At one time the suggestion of a horticultural show for Warrington would have been treated with levity, but on Saturday the Beautiful Warrington Society held its first show in Parr flail and peting a creditable display it was. While the number of com- exhibits was not large,tbe quality was uniformly good and there was every ground for encouragement. The society has made a good start and we have no ' doubt that next year the projectl will be taken up with greater enthusiasm. "How to keep cool " was the most pressing problem on Sunday, for we had the heat wave with a vengeance. Under such abnormal conditions open- air services were much more preferable to being cooped up in the stuffy atmosphere of places of worship, and this plan was followed by the people of at least one local church—St. Barnabas , Lovely-lane. Here the first part of the service was sung in the church, and thenthe choir and congregation adjourned to the churchyard, where the Vicar preached the sermon and several hymns were sung. The innovation, we hear, was much appreciated.

Wilson, Latchford ; Councillor Barton, Whitecross Ward; Councillor Wright, Bewsey Ward. * * * T Warrington gas consumer will rejoice , Hihe therto when the disposal of gas profits has been :considered he has been left out in the cold, the margin always going entirely to the relief of rates. Now, -110wever, the consumer's turn has come, and the Uouncil on Tuesday sanctioned a reduction in the price of gas of 2d. per 1,000 feet. Some members . thought the concession was not 'large enough, but we Inink the best frame of mind to adopt is to be thank- ful for small mercies. After all, the relief of rates has to be considered to some extent, even from the work- ingman's point of view, for when rates advance rents often go up too.

An interesting wedding was solemnized at Wargrave Church, Earlestown on Monday, when the contracting parties were Mr. Marshall Gandy, son of Mr. Win. Gandy, of Mersey-street. Warrington. and Miss Mabel Jones, of Queen-street, Earlestown. The ceremony was conducted by the Rev. A. Inglis of Winwick. The bride, who was given away by her uncle, Mr. A. Peake, of Earlestown, looked charming in a dress of embroidered white silk. She also wore a gold and emerald brooch and carried a bouquet,the gifts of the bridegroom. The bridesmaids were Miss Cissy Peake (cousin of the bride), Miss Amy Gandy and Miss Gertrude Gandy (sisters of the bridegroom), and they wore dresses of white silk, each also wearing a gold brooch and carrying a shower bouquet. the gifts of the bridegroom. Mr. Percy Bowcock Gandy (brother of the bridegroom), officiated as best man. The wedding party afterwards returned to the house of Mr. Peake, the bride's uncle, where a large number of guests we*e entertained. Later in the day the newly-married pair left for Scarborough, the bride's travelling dress being of reseda green. Many valuable presents were received.

THE HEALTH OF RUNCORN. The monthly meeting of the Runcorn Urban District Council was held on Wednesday, Mr. D. Bisbrown in the chair. Dr. Nl`Dougall (medical officer) reported that during August there were twenty-eight deaths, of which eight were due to diarrhoea and one to enteric fever. There were forty-eight births during the month.ln the corresponding month last year there were nineteen deaths and forty-eight births. On the recommendation of the Highways and Buildings Conimittee, it was resolved to pave with setts the portion of Canal-street from Wivern-place and Mason-street, at an estimated cost of £339, of which the Highfield Tannery Company agreed to pay £250, and the Australian Alum Company £5O. On the minutes of the Health Committee, Mr. Handley asked was nothing to be said to remove the state of unrest that existed in the town in regard to infectious diseases. Tell the public what steps had been taken, and were now being taken. Mr. Wisw all : We do not want to advertis ourselves. Mr. Handley No ; but simply allay the feeling. It was stated in committee that there were fewer notifica- tions last month than in August last year. Mr. Wiswall : Let sleeping dogs lie. Mr. Dean, chairman of the Health Committee, said at the last m.eeting there were no cases in the hospital,but on the previous day two strangers to the town told him the hospital was full. If it were so, he had no official 1 knowledge. The minutes were passed. I The resolution of the Chester County Council with ; regard to the Motor-car Acts Commission was submitted. Mr. Dean said motor-car dust did great harm to vegeta- tion, and if nothing were done to stop it, some means I would have to be devised to clear the dust from vegetables before being given to animals as food. Mr. Handley said a four-wheeled carriage for pleasure had to pay a licence of £2 2s. and a four wheeled motor-car only £l. These ought to be equalised at least. The subject was referred to the Highway Committee.

Tilston —Gill. A crowded congregation assembled at Holy Trinity Church to witness the marriage of Mr. Basil H. Tilston, Haseldene, Heswall, third son of Mr. Wm. Hawkins Tilston, and Miss Alice Gill, second daughter of Mr. Win. Gill, Winmarleigh-street. Considerable interest was shown in the ceremony, the bride and her parents being well known in Warrington. As the bride entered the church, Mr. NV. R. Randall, the organist, struck up the Bridal Chorus from " Lohengrin," and later played other appropriate music. The Rev. E. C. E. Carleton (vicar) was the officiating clergyman. The bride, who was given away by her father, looked charming in a dress of ivory satin de chine,with insertions and volants of Mechlin lace, whilst she also wore an embroidered tulle veil and wreath of white heather and orange blossom. Her jewels included a gold brooch with pearls and emeralds, a gift of the bridegroom. 'and her bouquet was of choice white flowers, also a gift of the bridegroom. The bridesmaids were Miss Clara Gill (sister of the bride), and Miss Irene Tilston (sister of the bridegroom), who were beautifully attired in ivory Chantilly lace robes of floral design, outlined with a bright silk effect, a touch of colour being introduced by long chine silk sashes. Their white crinoline hats were trimmed with chiffon and ostrich tips. They also wore brooches of pbarls, the gifts of the bridegroom, and carried bouquets of carnations. Mr. Richard Routledge, of Penketh,was the best man. The wedding party afterwards proceeded to the house of the bride's parents for the wedding breakfast, and later in the day Mr. and Mrs. Tilston left for the South of Ireland, where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling costume was of blue cloth, tailor made, with an embroidered collar of white cloth and moss green velvet, with a green feather hat. The happy pair were the recipients of an ex- ceptionally large number of handsome presents, includ- ing a dining-room clock from the employes of the Union Tanneries, Ltd. The bride's present to the bridegroom consisted of a travelling bag.

FACTORY ACT INFRINGEMENT. At Newton Petty Sessions on Thursday, Messrs. John Slee and Co., engineers,Earlestown, were summoned for an infringentent of the Factory Act.—lnspeotor Lewis said the proceedings were taken on one of the Inspector finding a led named Thomas Smith working in Messrs. Slee's works without being entered on the register of persons employed there. The law required that every youth employed for 7 days or more should have his name on the register and be certified by Dr. Watkins as fit for work. Failure to comply with this made the employer liable to a fine of £5. On the sth of July this lad was at work, and had been for six weeks without having been entered on the register at Slee said it was a clerk's mistake, and it was the first time in 25 years that he had had any complaint from the inspector It was quite an accidental omission.—Fined ss. and costs.

wife under a maintenance order.—Mr. J. W. Knowles who was instructed to prosecute,said the Bench granted Mrs.Grifhths a separation order last May. She had had one two years before, and with a view to getting back to her he reformed, went to chapel twice on a Sunday, and became a very good man for a time. But he was given to periodical drinking bouts, which were apt to last many months together. He began to drink again about the time of the election, last January, and had kept on continually since then. When in drink he became simply a madman, not responsible for what he did. At those times he always " went for "his wife— ' a most respectable woman, who had an income left her lby her late father. They had been well off, and would Ibe well off now, but for his drinking. The probability was that he would kill her one of these days. He had no right to go to her house, but he went and kicked at her door on the day named. She knew who it was, and she kept quiet. So he broke the front window, got in land damaged the pictures in the front room. If the I Bench would deal with the first charge he(Mr.Knowles) did not propose to go into the others. He asked the magistrates to bind him over under the Licensing Act, in two £lO or £5 sureties, or to send him to prison for I 6 months if he could not find sureties. Had he been convicted three times this year he would have asked them to send him to an Inebriates' Home ; they could not do that in the circumstances, but unless something could be done with him he would surely be a candidate for the galldpvs.—Evidence having been given, prisoner denied having been drunk and said he went to tell his wife that he was going abroad. As he got no answer he broke the window, got in and smashed four pictures which belonged to him—Prisoner was bound over,him- self in £2O and two sureties of £lO each to be of good behaviour for 12 months, the alternative being six months' imprisonment.

ST, HELENS.

ST. HELENS APPOINTMENTS.

-•--- - The St. Helens Corporation and the Education Com- mittee have made a large number of appointments during the past few weeks, among which are the appoint- ment of Dr. Frances Harper, London, to be lady assist- ant medical officer ; Mr. H. Butler' Liverpool, to be art master ; Miss M, A. Ormand. Earlestown, to be cookery and laundry assistant at the Gamble Institute; Mr. J. H. Whiteley, Southport, to be French and English master ; and Mr. P. A. Morris, Salford, to be instructor for the manual training school. The lElementary Education Committee have reaffirmed the appointments sent back from the last monthly meeting, viz., Mr. T. G. Henderson, Lancaster, to be head master of Allanson-street Council School, at a salary of £l5O a year, and the appointment of Mr. T. Phillips, Windle School, to be head master of either the mixed depart- ment or the higher elementary department of the new Cowley Council Schools when they are erected. ST. HELENS COUNCILLORS' RETIREMENT.

At the conclusion of the Irish demonstration, held at the Volunteer Hall, St. Helens, on Monday, which was attended by a good number of priests, Father Barry announced that Dr. P. J. O'Keefe, who had presided at the meeting, would shortly retire from the Town Council. He also announced that Mr. P. J. O'Brien would be a candidate for the Central Ward in opposition to Councillor Walsh, And urged the Catholics to stand by Councillor J. A. Collins, who was threatened with opposition in liardshaw Ward. A year ago Dr. O'Keefe threw in his lot with the local Labour party, and took a prominent part in the return of Mr. T. Glover to Parliament When the Government brought in their Education Bill Dr. O'Keefe felt compelled, as a Catholic, to take a stand against the Bill, and it is evi- dent that the local Catholic AsSociationhave decided to run their own candidates and safeguard as far as possible their own interests. ST. HELENS NATIONALISTS.

- - On Monday night Mr. W. J. Duffy, the member of Parliament for South Galway, was the grincipal speaker at a demonstration of Gatholic Irishmen at St. Helens. He appealed to them to continue to give generous and whole-hearted support to the Nationalist cause. The leaders of the Irish party had drilled the public opinion of this country into an acknowledgment that Home Rule was the only possible solution for the Irish 1 National difficulty. The democracy of the land, as represented by various sections of the Liberal party in 1 the House of Commoas,had submitted a proposition that really covered the whole demand for Home Rule. They admitted that the worst possible form of government was that carried on in Ireland, and no responsible Government in any country, mach less England, com- mitting itself to that proposition could stop until they found a way of solving the difficulty to the satisfaction of the people qf Ireland. Having taken up the ques- tion, there was no stopping; they must at °rice con- cede to Ireland that measure of Home Rule which was enshrined in the bill of that great and far-seeing states- man Mr. Gladstone. (Applause.) Sir Antony Mac Donnell spoke of it in terms that showed it was there, and there it should remain. The speaker was confident that Home Rule would soon come.

Si.. HELENS LABOUR DEMONSTRATION.

- -At a great Labour demonstration at St. Helens on Wednesday night I\lr. Seddon, the member for the Newton division, moved a resolution urging the necessity for all workers to join their trade union to secure better protection and to help in returning a larger number of independent representatives to all governing bodies. He reminded them that the Labour party had reduced the time working men had to wait for compensation after accidents, and said that had the Labour party done nothing else they had justified their existence as an independent force by forcing the hands of the Government to do justice to the British working man. They had forced upon the Government, too, the necessity of feeding school children. INIr. Thomas Glover, the member for St. Helens, said it seemed strange that in the twentieth century they had to urge upon workers the necessity of combining together. The improved conditions of labour were due to trade organisations. Capitalists organised to protect their own interests, and if it was right for capitalists to be organised, it was high time workers combined to protect their rights. Nfr. Wardle, the member for Stockport, said they could not emphasise too much the necessity for and the power that lay in trade unionism. Strikes, lockouts, and industrial i wars were not all there was n trade unionism. Was it not protection to the worker, and did it not act as his providence ? For the first time in the history of England they had a real live Labour party in the House of Commons, and they had great influence upon their fellows Cu the Continent and in America. He hoped they would see all Labour members under one banner

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

-- St. Peter's Men's Bible Class.

An open service in connection with St.Peter' Men's Bible Class was held on Sunday afternoon in the church. The organ recitals by Mr. F. IL Darby constituted a great feature, and Miss L. Pllnston's rendition of " The Lost Chord '! was excellent. Miss Maud Hill sang well in " Ave Maria" and " The Holy City." During the , afternoon the Bev. Henry Russell delivered an address on" Music," A collection ..vas taken on behalf of the Church Pastoral Aid Society. Railway Servants' Church Parade. The annual parade in connection with the Warrington Branch of the Amalgamated Society of Railway Servants was held on Sunday afternoon. A fairly large number assembled on the Town Hall lawn, and, headed by their band, proceeded to St. Paul's Church. The service was conducted by the Vicar (the Rev. \V. Bracecamp), who delivered an address, and the choir igave an excellent , rendering of the anthem, "The King of Love my . shepherd is,', Mr. Wilkes presiding at the organ. A collection was taken on behalf of the Society's Orphan and Benevolent Fund. In the evening the members ' attended service at the Working Men's Mission. Wesleyan Anniversary,

The anniversary services in connection with Bold— street Wesleyan Church were held on Sunday, and were ' well attended. Two addresses were • delivered in the morning and evening by the Rev. W. F. Mayer, of Bootle, to large congregations. The anthems, "0, Saviour of the world ' and "Judge me, 0 God," were rendered in admirable style by the choir, Mr. F. Hick- man presiding at the organ. At the close of each ser- vice, a collection was taken in aid of the trust funds, and ealized a good sum. Church of the Sacred Heart.—Choir Trip. The members of the Sacred Heart choir spent an enjoyable afternoon on Saturday when they visited Southport. Ihe afternoon was occupied in visiting the arcades motoring on the marine drive, rowing on the lake, L. At half-past four the party adjourned to Churchtown Gardens where tea was partaken of. A visit was then paid to the palm houses the Museum and other places of interest in the gardens. The return journey was commenced at 9.5, the choir arriving at Warrington shortly after ten o'clock. The weather was splendid, and the outing was voted a great success, thanks to the admirableearrangments of the choirmaster and conductor (Jr. John Gibbins). 'Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

[By " Ovalist.] Rugby football appears to have at last been dragged out of the slough into which,during the last few years, it has been cast by the well-intentioned but ill-advised actions of the controlling authority. The new rules give promise of improving the game almost out of recognition and those Warrington enthusiasts who were the first to advocate the alterations are now wearing contented smiles and indulging in expres- sions indicative of their satisfaction. They have every reason to feel not a little proud. The Northern Union game, as the result of the ineffectual muddlings of the Committee, was going down. It had been rendered unattractive by reason of its degeneracy into a long and monotonous succession of " scrums " and the spectators were crying out against it when Warrington stepped into the breach with proposals which have lifted it on to an altogether higher plane and made it indeed a game worth watch- ing. It is now fest—much faster than in the old days —and open to a degree beyond even the most sanguine predictions of the advocates of the change. The new touch rule caused some little confusion and though thekplayers as a rule avoided the pit-falls which it created there were occasional lapses of memory. Burgess in particular forgot himself and his careless- ness was the chief incident which led up to Warring- ton's opening try. Warrington were attacking when Burgess sought with a huge punt to relieve. The ball, however, found touch and the Leigh line was placed in even greater danger. Burgess made a mark near the posts and his kick again found touch. The players were ordered back to where Burgess kicked and from the scrummage Brooks crossed over. The forgetfulness of the players could, however, be excused, especially when the referee him- self ruled wrongly on the question. On one occasion he ordered a scrum near the Warrington line, when it should have been at the other end of the field, and Warrington would have been unjustly penalized had not Fish convinced him of his error.

The " play the ball " rule has perhaps more to do with the altered state of the game than any other of the drastic changes made by the N.U. Committee. By its operation the number of scrummages is reduced by fifty per cent., and the game is consequently more continuous. Pessimists arguing against the proposal urged that it would lead to rough play, but of this there was not the slightest suspicion on Saturday. In fact one of the principal features of the game was the absence of anything savouring of brute force. The tackling was vigorous but, generally speaking, strictly fair, and if all games are fought as clearly the sus- pension list be extremely light. Now as to the play and the weather.

It was hot. This remark applies almost equally to the game as to the weather, but is intended as a comment on the latter. On Friday perspiring pedes- trians gasped and mopped their heated brows, whilst ice and all things cool were at a premium. Persons whose business it is to study the weather, discovered that it was the hottest day we have experienced for something like thirteen years. Saturday was hotter still ! The sun shone fiercely from a cloudless sky, and at the football match the unique spectacle was presented of spectators beneath the shade bf the grand stand discarding coat and hat while watching the " winter game." When it was uncom- fortably warm even to remain quiescent it was not to be wondered that after half an hour's hard work the players looked limp and exhausted, and welcomed every stoppage of the game with evident relief. The only matter which occasioned surprise was that they stuck it so well and it speaks volumes for their preliminary training that many (lid not collapse altogether. --- As above stated the game was fast and entertaining though it was very largely confined to foot-work. Passing movements were at a discount and this was to be regretted, as, after all, nothings more attrac- tive and more exciting than to watctr the ball travel from hand to hand amongst a well-drilled set of backs. Only once in the afternoon were the spectstors treated to a tit-bit of this description and this was provided by Warrington. The play was proceeding near the Leigh line when Lees secured the ball which passed, through six pairs of hands and, ending at Brooks, was grounded over the line. The combination , was absolutely irresistible and the Warringtonians indulged in a pronounced chuckle at the expense of their friends. Unfortunately the effort was, so to speak, a mere flash in the pan and nothing of a like nature being again forthcoming, Warrington did not run up so big a score as their supporters confidently looked for. To come to the players and starting with the backs, Berry was one of the stars of the afternoon. His fielding was a thing to be wondered at especially as he hardly misjudged the ball in spite of the glaring sun. He kicked with great judgment and his tackling was effective. Altogether he was never in better foi in and so long as he keeps off the injured list Warrington need net be apprehensive as to the rear line of the defence. Another defender who stood out prominently was Taylor who, still as cool as ever—in the sense that he did not lose his head—brought off some brilliant saves when Berry was hard pressed Taylor also had particularly hard lines in not placing a try to his credit. During some loose play he stopped up and dribbling through the Leigh backs raced for the line with the ball at his toes. He had nearly achieved his object when he tripped and fell and from his lowly position watched the ball roll slowly over the dead line. The surprise of the afternoon was Brooks, who only stepped into the team at the last minute when he took the place of Harris. He worked like the pro- verbial nigger and where the fight was thickest there he was to be found. He is not generally regarded as a speedy man but the way be raced past Neville—a sprinter with a reputation—when he scored his second try was a revelation. Lees also opened the eyes of those who have questioned his right to partner Hockenhall. In fact he was even better than the old county man, who did some smart work and also trotted out some very poor stuff. The disappointment of the day was Fish, not that his play was so bad but one is accustomed to expect some- thing above the average from "Jack," and on Satur- day he was, if anything, a little below the mark. Whether he was oppressed by the heat it is difficult to say, but lie did not exert himself sufficiently and time after time S. Johnson completely beat him, a thing which few men can do. Toi Johnson's credit it must be said that he played well /Ind it is safe to predict that ere the season is much further advanced he will have placed a respectable number of tries to his credit. Of the forwards little need be said. To a man they played up well but if distinctions must be made the palm should go to Heath and Thomas whose dribbling powers are adapted to the new game. The front rank I would do well to study more closely the art of dribbling I and if they do this they will be equal to any six whom they will have to meet.

The result of the match was somewhat disappoint- ing, as undoubtedly Warrington ought to have won. At one time they led by eight points, when for some unaccountable reason they " eased up " towards the close, a fact which Leigh ..vere not blow to take advan- tage of, with the result that a draw was declared. It would be well for them to remember in the future not to !slacken their efforts until the whistle has gone, for as Leigh showed, a great deal can happen in the last few minutes, and the least carelessness might on another occasion result in defeat.

Warrington to-day (Saturday) entertain Hull on the Wilderspool enclosure and on paper should register a victory, hut one never knows. Hull, no matter what their previous form has been, generally manage to give Warrington a good game and there is every pros- pect of an attractive afternoon's play. Warringt'm place the same team in the field as that which did duty against Leigh and this can be regarded as the strongest thirteen which could be played—provided the left wing shows a little improvement, especially in defence. The team selected is : Berry, full back ; Fish, Isherwood, Taylor and Brooks, threequarters ; Hockenhall and Lees, halves ; Preston, Thomas, Boardman, Naylor, Harmer and Heath, forwards.

The" A "team have opened the season well and in a friendly with Widnes Rangers on Saturday had everything their own way. If their present form can be maintained there is every prospect of last year's excellent record being equalled. The second string includes several very promising youngsters whose effort stamp them as future first team reserves of a high standard. One is Boardman a youthful forward who promises to faithfully uphold the traditions associated with the name which be bears.

Bowling.

Bowlers of Warrington will this (Saturday) afternoon almost naturally gravitate in the direction of Bank Quay where a battle of local giants is promised. The attraction is afforded in connection with the War- rington Works Association the finals of whose team competitions will be decided. In the contest proper an exciting struggle is to be looked for. Neither of the two teams—Flether's and Ryland's (No. I)—were expected to survive the semi-final, both having to meet combinations whose efficiency had in the past secured them the championship, but each rose manfully to the occasion and delighted and surprised their anxious supporters by emerging triumphant from the ordeal.

In the first mateh of the semi-final, which was decided last Saturday, there was a sporting finish which delights the heart of the trundler, the issue being in doubt, so to speak, until the last wood to be bowled lay in close proximity to the block. The teams were P. and K., last years champions, and who greatly fancied their prospects, and Rylands (No. 1), a combination who had figured with consistent success throughout the progress of the tournament. P. and K. started with flying colours, Gleave registering a victory of 15-7 over Holbrook, but a foretaste of the excititing struggle which was to follow was immediately afforded when Roughley, running away from H. Bmnett succeeded in reversing the lead. Then followed a game brimful of excitement. Alternate)y each team claimed the advantwge, until after seven players had tried con- clusions, when P. .and K. had apparently established themselves in a strong position by cla,ining atotal score of 94 as against SO compiled by their opponents.

Rvlands were however by no means beaten, and led by A. Shaw they showed that P. and K. were not out of the wood. In seven ends Shaw led by 10-1, and a like number ends later he had,won 15-5, and the totals stood at 95-99. Dismay was occasioned by J. Shaw's non- success against R. Rothwell, and the P. and K. star was once more in the ascendant. Rylands, however, still numbered some resolute men amongst their ranks, and Wells after a long fight with Ball was accounted the victor. Still P. and K. held the advantage, and though Wood succeeded in reducing their lead by one point two marks separated the teams when the last two men were on the green. J. Worsley after an indifferent start ran to 11-S, and P. and K. were almost beginning to cheer over victory when disaster came. Three points only were required to send them on to the final, but they were never registered. Monks found his length and with well placed woods crept from S-11 to 15-11. Worsley made a game fight but Monks appreciated his responsibility, and right manfully did he discharge it. He was the hero of the match, and, in fact, of the after- noon.

Fletcher's were able to record a &ore crushing victory over L. and N.-W. Opening with a win of 15-5 they maintained their lead until the fourth game when R. Marrow succeeded in wresting it from them. When the match was half over they were still behind but they were not to be denied. Wood stepped into the breach and winning 15-8 placed his side a few points ahead. Having again tested the joys of being able to regard a balance of points in their in their favour. Fletcher's did not allow any chances to slip by unobserved and in the end won by 161 to 145. In the final they will need to bring all their resources into play to defeat Rylands and though to prophesy is risky I do not think they will succeed. However time alone will show whether I am right and in any event which everside loses I am certain it will not be disgraced.

Rylands are also in high favour as prospective winners of the consolation competition though on Saturday they only beat Monks Hall by five points. However a miss is as good as a mile and to-day they intend putting up a good fight. The other matches decided last week were between Whitecross (No. 1) and Greenings, and Orford Tannery and Castle Rubber and were both some- what one sided, the first-named teams in each case winning with ease.

The semi-final and final for the consolation prizes will also be decided this afternoon when in the first the teams will bo Lokgford v. Orford Tannery, and Rvlands (1) and Whitecross (No. 1 The prizes will be presented at the conclusion of the play. The proceed- ings will commence at two o'clock.

Swimming.

An interesting stage in connection with the Warring- ton Works Squadron competition for the Gilbert Greenall challenge shield was reached on Thursday, when the semi-final was decided in the Legh-street baths. Fletcher's and Pearsm and Knowles were the first to meet, and Pearson and Knowles were by far the better team and won by a length. Corporation and Monks Hall had a better fight, but Monks Hall claimed the victory by a few yards. According to the times which were registered Pearson and Knowles ought to win the final, but in any case it will be a race well worth watching.

There was an additional item of interest at Thurs- day's proceedings in the shape of a polo match between Warrington and Liverpool City in connection with the second division of the Liverpool and District League. In the first few minutes Pemberton got away and passed • to Allen whose shot just tipped the bar. Play in the first half was fast and interesting and each end was visited in quick succession. C. Ashton repeatedly had hard lines and half tints arrived with no score. In the second stage the game was evenly contested but Liver- pool were quicker to take advantage of openings and Vates,for themscored the only tw4 goals n f the match. The score hardly rvresented the play and Warrington had to thank their weak shooting for their defeat.

cycling. Saturday was observed by the members of the Warrington Cycling Club as President's Day and Ladies' Day. The weather was ideal for the fixture and 3:5 members and lady friends turned up at Chester, where the journey was continued by steamer to Eccleston Ferry. Here an excellent tea was provided, after which , the health of the President, Mr. John Naylor, was ! heartily drank. Both the outward and the return journeys wore enlivened by songs, glees, &c.. given by the members of the W.C.C.Pierrot Troupe. Warrington was reached again about 10 o'clock after ons of t 13,0 most enjoyable outings of the season.

Cricket.

Interest in cricket is now almost blow vanishing point but it is lo ha regretted that the Warrington Club is closing the season by indifferent displays. On Saturday they were soundly trounced by Preston who rather spoiled the record of one of the most successful seasons in the history of the Club.

Walker's Brewery closed their season on Saturday with an easy victory over Lostock Gralam. After dis- missing their opponents for 12 runs they piled up s. score of SI, J. Dargan being the highest scorer with 21 it is also worthy of note that he took five wickets for two runs.

DEATH OF JUDGE BOWEN ROWLANDS.

We announce with deep regret— which will be shared by many of our readers—the death of his Honour Judge Bowen P.owlands, K.C., the esteemed County Court Judge of Circuit No. 7., which took place in London on Tuesday evening after a com- paratively brief illness. Of late his Honour's health had been indifferent and his duties had been die- charged at various times by members of the Liver- pool Bar, including Mr. Hanbury Aggs, Mr. Cuthbert Smith, Mr. Stewart Brown, and Mr. W. H. Oulton. For some little time he had retired for a recreative rest to his London residence, 00, Kensington- mansions, where he passed away. By many Warring- ton people the .news of his death will be received with genuine regret for <luring the six years that he had occupied the Bench at the War- rington County Curt his Honour had by his tact, courtesy and geniality endeared himself to those whose lot it was to be associated with him in the dis- charge of his arduous duties. He 'was a thorough gentleman and an able and painstaking judge, ready to hear with the utmost patience any case brought before him—no matter how tedious. He was of a kindly and sympathetic disposition, conscientious to a degree, and all who appeared at his Court had confi- dence that his decisions were always given with a desire to be scrupulously fair. Judge Rowlands was born in 1839, and was the eldest son of the late Thomas Rowraiids, J.P., of Glen- over, Pembroke. He was educated at Jesus College, Oxford, and graduated as B.A. in 1857, and took his M.A. in 1809. He was called to the bar in 1871, and was made a Q.C.'in January, 1881, and a bencher of his Inn in April of the same year. Judge Rowlands was formerly leader of the South \Vales and Chester Circuit, and was a lay member (by nomination of the Catholic Bishops) of the Catholic Schools Committee, and a J.P. for Cardigan, Haverfordwest, and Cheshire. In July, 1883, he successfully contested, in the Liberal , interest, the Parliamentary vacancy in Cardiganshire„ I which constituency he represented till July 1895. The late Judge had been recorder of Swanseasince 1893, and Judge of the County Court Circuit No. 7 since November, 1900. Judge Rowlands held courts at Altrincham, Birkenhead, Leigh, N.orthwich, Warring- ton, Winsford, and Runcorn, with. jurisdiction in bankruptcy at Birkenhead and Warrington. Amongst other positions of dignity held by him, and filled with great satisfaction, was the n.lastership of Gray's Inn Library, his extensive .acquaintance with legal know- ledge having marked him out for the position. An enthusiastic educationist, he was nominated by the Roman Catholic Bishop as a layman on the Catholic ;Education Council.

Railway Clerks' Association. A meeting of the members of the Railway Clerks' Association was held at the Royal Court Hotel, Rylands-street, on Monday night; Alderman W. J. West, ex-Mayor of Battersea, described the work done by railway clerks, and what the association had done f w their benefit. He appealed to those who were not already members of the association to join the ranks, and by so doing help forward reforms which were greatly needed. They wanted their clerks to have justice, which up to the present they had not got. The only thing they could do to help themselves was by co-operation, and he appealed to them to do their best in that direction. The association wanted to obtain a living wage for the clerks, and their pro- gramme was that the minimum in London should be 30s. a week, and in the country from 263. to 28s. a week.

HISTORIC HEALING. -

Ideals of Ancient Greece Realised In Zam-Buk—" Back to Nature !"

The story of how the proprietors of Zarn-Buk were induced to seek for and perfect such a healing balm, and the ideals which they kept before them throughout the years of experimentation which preceded its intro- duction to the public, provides interesting and instruc- tive reading. In the first place,it was recognized that modern oint- ments, salves, and liniments were failing to meet the needs of the people because they were made up of rancid animal fats or mineral products, These ingre- dients are too coarse to do the average skin any good at all, and some are so unsuitable for their purpose as to be really dangerous. At the outset, therefore it was decided that no unnatural ingredient should be used. Nature it was argued, had herself given to man the instinct to rub a place that hurts, and she should provide the ingredients. Along this line much encouragement was gleaned from the customs of ancient Greece. The splendid athletes and gladiators of that day, in particular, used healing oils and balms regularly, and they were invari- ably prepared from vegetable products. The housewife or breadwinner of to-day, who needs a bandy healer at home almost as much as the ancient Greek did after his mad chariot races and fierce contests, would do well to follow his example and stick close to Nature. This is exactly what you do if you pin your faith to Zam-Buk, as hundreds of thousands of people in all parts of the world are doing to-day. In the production of Zarn-Bak all animal fats, mineral poisons, and doubtful substances have been rigidly avoided, and the ideals of Ancient Greece have been realised by strict adherence to the motto : "Back to Nature." Certain rich herbs and shrubs were taken, and from these were extracted pure gums and juices possessing I the various medicinal properties necessary to make up a perfect skin-healer. The correct combination and concentration of these essences was only secured after lengthy experiments, but when Zam-Buk was finally put upon the market, in the closing years of last century, honest effort met with its reward in the hearty reception which everywhere greeted the new healing balm. Zam-Buk met a need which every householder had felt for decades.and to-day there are few indeed who do not know its purity and power—its excellence as a handy healer for the cuts, bruis3s, burns, and scalds that are of everyday occurrence, and its wonderful curative effect in cases of rashes, eruptions, and skin diseases. Keep a box handy in our home—the 2s. 9d. size contains nearly four times the is. lid. Of all chemists, or post free from the Zam-Buk Co., 4, Red Cross-street, London, E.C.

EX-MAYOR'S TRAGIC END.

• Atk, " ,

,

At the inquest on Wednesday on Mr. Fred Seymour Morgan, ex-Mayor of Winchester, who was on Tuesday found dead in his room from a bullet wound, it was stated that the deceased had much changed since his mayoralty, and that he had lately been considerably depressed, never seeming the same since an attack of influenza. The head constable said he was worrying over financial matters. A bullet from a revolver had penetrated the brain. The jury returned a verdict of " Suicide while of unsound mind." In a case at Scarborough on Wednesday, in which 'George Cass, aged nine, a donkey-boy, was summoned for sticking a 4-inch pin into a donkey on Scarborough sands, Inspector Jowett said the practice:of sticking pins and darning needles into donkeys to make them run had become very prevalent. He agreed to the case being withdrawn on the boy's father undertaking to chastise him

THE MAYOR'S AUDITOR.

SHOULD HE BE PAID?

Interesting Discussion at the Town Council Meeting.

Thu interesting question as to whether it is legal for a member of the Town Council to receive payment for his work as Mayor's auditor was raised at the meeting of the Warrington Town Council on Tuesday evening. The Finance and Estate Committee reported the receipt of the following report signed by the Mayor's Auditor and by one of the elective auditors, which they ordered to be entered on the minutes : Warrington, 17th August, 1906. To the Mayor, Alder- men and Burgesses of the County Borough of Warring- ton. Gentlernen,—Borough Audit.—We beg to report that we have audited the accounts of the various departments of the Corporation for the year ending I 31st March, 1906, and certify them to be correct. The deeds relating to the properties in possession of the 'Corporation have been produced to us. We desire to place on record that we find the accounts kept in a very satisfactory manner.—Yours faithfully, W. J. WILSON, Mayor's Auditor ; LAURENCE BOLTON, elective auditor. The report of Mr. E. England, elective auditor (which has already appeared in the "Examiner ") was also read and ordered to be entered on the minutes.

Alderman BENNErr, in moving the confirmation of the minutes of the Finance Committee, said Mr. England's report was a long one, but it contained many points of interest, and he called the special attention of the Council to it because coming from an independent official like Mr. England it was not without interest to the inhabitants of the town generally. First, as to property purchasing, Mr. England said :—" I have examined the deeds and ascertained the sums paid for the various properties bought by the Corporation during the past twenty years. I am of opinion that on the whole the purchases are judicious ones, and in the best interests of the town. These properties have not only included those required for street-widening purposes, but also many 'slum ' properties, which very properly have been and are being demolished." Alluding to the question of " Horse hire, cartage, conveyances, &c.," Mr. England wrote :—" In going through the various accounts, ono cannot help being struck with thelarge amount paid for the hire of horses, carts, and cabs. Two departments alone have spent £1,190 12s. 10d. and £l,OOO 12s. 3d. respectively for these items, and altogether over £3,000 is spent year after year in this respect. To those who understand finance they will easily see that .£3,000, capitalized to a Corporation, means a very large amount. I have, therefore, come to the conclusion that not only could a considerable sum be saved to the town, but employment found under superior conditions than at present exists for men who would become Corporation employes. In addition, con- siderable time would be saved as well to various depart- ments in preparing specifications, kc., for work required to be done, and also a good deal of labour saved to the Council in considering tenders and accepting contracts." The work would also be better and more efficiently done. The matter was, said Alderman Bennett, one of some importance, and personally he was very much in symplthy with the general idea which underlay Mr. England's remarks. The only question was whether horse hire could be more economically managed by the Corporation than it was under the present system. He was told that on one or two occasions the committee I bad tried to run the department with their own horses, but had not seen any advantage from it. However, it was a matter which warranted further inquiry. Certainly the principle was sound. but the great difficulty was that the work of the Corporation fluctuated. Then Mr. England alluded to the financial status of the Corpora- tion in the following terms :—"The financial position of the Corporation is undoubtedly sound. It has been my lot and privilege for many years to have been in close touch with public men and public offices of other towns, and have no hesitation in saying that the War- rington Corporation financially compares very favour- ably with others. Most of the undertakings are in a remunerative condition and various economies have been effected. The policy of your predecessors and of some of you, therefore, in purchasing these and also in introducing new undertakings, and thus bringing them under public control and management instead of being in private hands, has thus proved to have been a wise and sound one and has contributed greatly in many ways to the prosperity of the town." That opinion was not without its value. The Council had been criticised unmercifully, and at times unfairly, in reference to many of these matters in the past, and such an opinion from a man who was not, he believed, at the outset pre-disposed to view the affairs of the Council too favourably, but who said the Corporation had done well, was something to be proud of, (Hear, hear.) Councillor FULTON SMITH seconded the confirmation of the minutes. Councillor STOTT said he wished to refer to the report of the auditors. The MAYOft : What is the use of bothering? They are auditors—not critics.

Councillor Stott said they owned their horses in the nightsoil department, and it paid them, and if it paid them to do it in that department why should it not pay in another ? For permanent work they ought, he con- tended, to engage their own horses. The only argument offered against that course was that when the Corpora- tion owned horses the animals used to die. (Laughter.) It appeared that they must never die_ under private ownership. (Laughter.) He should like a report on the matter to be presented either to the Council or to the General Purposes Committee. Councillor Lord rose to speak, but . .

The MAYOR interposed, rind said he did not know whether be would be in order in doing so. Councillor LORD : But the matter is mentioned on the minutes. If you allow an Alderman to speak on the auditors' report you should allows Councillor to do so. The MAYOR : But it is only one man's opinion how to carry on the business of the Corporation. Councillor PEMBERTON : The matter is on the agenda. The INIAYort : It does not matter whether it is or not. Go ahead. I don't want to sit here two hours discussing anyone's opinion as to whether the Council is right or wrong. Councillor LORD then raised a protest against the payment of the Mayor's auditor, and asked if the accounts totalling £22,000 which had been passed for payment by the Finance Committee includod payment of the Mayor's auditor.

The DEPUTY TOWN CLERK: Yes. The MAYOR: That is all right. I employ him and he ought to be paid. Councillor LORD said he understood the payment waf contrary to the provisions of "The Municipal Corpora- tions Act, 1892-3," which distinctly stated that no member of the Council should receive a profit for any services rendered. They had fought against this in the past, and they must be consistent, and oppose the payment again, lie moved as an amendment that the payment of the Mayor's Auditor be not agreed to. The MAYOR : You are out of order. The amount is paid to the three auditors and they divided it amongst themselves. Councillor LORD : I understand they receive the amount separately. The MAYOR : The Deputy Town Clerk tells me that it is perfectly legal, and that it is always done. Councillor LORD : it is a matter. of opinion.... .

He is not paid under the Municipal Corporations Act. He does not receive a penny, nor do the other auditors. He is paid under The Public Health Act. He is not an officer appointed by the Corporation, but by the Mayor. Councillor LORD : And the Mayor is the head of the Corporation. The DEPUTY TOWN CLERK : He is the head of the Corporation, and it especially provides in The Munici- pal Corporations Act that the Mayor's auditor shall be a member of the Council. He is not pflid under The Municipal Corporations Act at all. Councillor LORD : Jam very much obliged to the Deputy Town Clerk for what he has just told us. I understand that the Mayor's Auditor is paid under the Public Health Act, 1875,—passed prior to the Municipal Corporation Act,—which distinctly states that" where an urban authority are the Council of a borough they shall, subject to the provisions of this Act, exercise and execute their powers, authorities and duties under this Act according to the laws for the time being in I force, with respect to municipal corporations in Eng- land," so that " The Public Health Act "is really sub- servient to "The Municipal Corporations Act." The Municipal Corporations Act " is more recent, and it distinctly states that "a person shall be disqualified from being elected and from being a councillor if and while he holds any office or place of profit other than that of the Mayor or sheriff, or has employment with by, or on behalf of the Council." I think that is very clear. It is certainly very clear to my mind, and must protest against the payment to the Mayor's auditor. I shall move an amendment to this minute by a reduction of the amount set aside to be paid to the Mayor's Auditor. Councillor PEMBERTON seconded, and said there was not the slightest doubt, with all due respect to the Deputy Town Clerk, that no member of the Council could receive any monies or profit from the Corpora- tion. If the Mayor liked to give his auditor payment out of his own pocket, he could do so. That was a different matter altogether. (Laughter.) The MAYOR : I don't know whether that would be legal. Would it ? Councillor PEMBERTON : Oh, yes. The MAvort. : lam afraid you would find chapter and verse for that—that it was bribery and corruption. (More laughter.) Councillor PF.MBERTON : Not at all. doubt that "The Municipal Corporations Act "was very distinct, and that no money belonging to the ratepayers should be paid to a member of the Council. He must not receive any profit. Councillor WRIGHT contended that the matter was ; really worth further consideration, but at the present time he felt inclined to vote for the minutes as they i stood. In future he should advise his friends on his right (Councillors Lord and Pemberton) to take the auestion up at some other time. The payment might well be allowed to pass on that occasion, at any rate, and they would take the matter into consideration before there was another audit. The Mayor's Auditor had done his work well and should have any pay he was entitled to. The Mayor's Auditor had always been paid in Warrington. It was all very well to say that this Objection was raised as a protest and for principle. Perhaps it was. At any rate, ha was prepared to sup- port the passing of the minutes. Jolley that they had received moneys improperly, for acting as Mayor's auditors. Legal opinion was taken, and it was found' to be a fact that the payment was perfectly in order. On those terms he accepted office. When the agitation commenced he (Alderman Bennett) raised the point again, and it was held most emphatically that the payment was perfectly legal, and that opinion had now been fortified by the Deputy Town Clerk. He believed he was stating the fact when he declared that , Mr. Whittle had taken counsel's opinion, and that he was also fortified by the opinion of the Association of Municipal Corporations. It was idiotic to expect a man to give the necessary time to the duties of the office without being paid for it; it was absurd to expect him to give three or four weeks of his time without receiving anything for the work. He (Alderman Bennett) could not afford to do so, and he did not think any other business man could. It was absolutely monstrous to expect a man, in addition to his ordinary work as a councillor—with the addition of late years of the work connected with the Electricity and Tramways Dapartments—to do the work he had named effectually unless he was paid.He spoke of Alderman Fairclough with the greatest respect and admiration, and he was sorry he was not present owing to ill-health, but the Deputy- Mayor on being approached, very properly and sensibly said "I don't object to filling the position nominally, but lam not going to do the work," and the other auditors did the work and got the fee. Councillor Wilson had done the work, and it was contrary to all reason and fair play to expect him to do it for nothing. The demands of big municipalities on the time of Councillors were becoming so great and serious that it might eventually become a question whether men could do the ordinary Council work without remuneration. Until they had a new race of philanthropists rich men who had no living to make, they would either have to be satisfied with a reasonable modicum of time given by members of the Corporation or place the affairs of Conn- roils on an entirely different footing. (Hear, hear.) He had alway said the law was a "bass " (Laughter). In this ease, however, he believed the law was on their side, so that it was not a " bass "for once. (Laughter). At the present time there was considerable agitation as to the importance of the duties of auditors and the committee on Municipal Trading had made some very sensible recommendations to the effect that qualified professional accountants should do the work, and be considered officials of the Corporation. That, he believed, would be the ultimate solution. On a vote being taken only Councillors Lord, Pemberton, Cunnington, Wilding and Ashcroft voted for the amendment which was therefore lost. The minutes were then approved.

The Late Mr. Henry Taylor.

Speaking of the late Mr. H. S. Taylor, the " Holy Trinity Parish Magazine " says :—" He always had work on hand and was always ready to take up new work. In addition to his work as Police Court Missionary, to which he gave his whole heart, he did a great deal for our parish, as indeed he did for other parishes in the town as occasion required. He was, however, chiefly interested in the Factory Yard Mission, and the general oversight that be took, of the mission services there will make Ms death severely felt in that part of our work. That he should so suddenly be taken away in the midst of the work that he loved cannot be otherwise regarded than as a gain to himself, , though a source of sorrow to us and especially to his poor wife, whose health renders her not very fit to battle with the world alone. That God may comfort and support her will be, we are sure, the prayer of those who have known her excellent husband.

SHOCKING STREET ACCIDENT.

Henry Pennington, aged seventy-two, of 39, Water- street, died in the Providence Hospital, St. Helens, on Saturday afternoon, after being injured a week previously. Pennington was infirm and very short- sighted and deaf. On Saturday, August 25th, he was crossing the road in Tontine-street, and failed to notice a milk float belonging to James Whalley, of 185, Clipsley-lane, Haydock. The shaft struck the old man down and fractured his ribs, and, after lingering a week death took place at the hospital.*

er, Saturday, September 8, 1906. SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

HOW TO KEEP OFF SUNSTROKE.

Whether on land, on the hills, or by the sea- shore, the colour of the outer garments, says Science Siftinge, materially affects body and brain when they are exposed to the rays of the sun. An orange-coloured lining to the hat will remove much of the danger of sunstroke. The red ray is the one which is responsible for many sunstrokes. When the brain is protected flora this pernicious ray, one of the chief sources of danger is removed.

TURPENTINE FUMES FOR INFLUENZA. The United States Consul to Frankfort, Ger- many, says that during an epidemic of influenza prevalent in the vicinity it was found that work- men in a certain watch factory, exposed to evaporating oil of turpentine, seemed to be pro- tected from the disease. Since then oil of tur- pentine has been always evaporated in that fac- tory upon a stove, and not a case of influenza has ever occurred there. This preventive mea- sure is successfully employed in dwellings, and the inhaling of watery vapour with oil of tur- pentine is said to act favourably on the affected respiratory organs.

===== 0002601_19060908_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
LONG LIFE FOR THE EARTH.

That the earth will be habitable for 100,000,000 years to come is the belief of Dr. Thomas C. Chamberlin, a well-known geologist. He de. clares that climatic phenomena and temperature conditions of the last 100,000,000 years warrant him in offering his optimistic prophecy. His basis for the theory of perpetuity for human life is his own "planetesimal hypothesis" that the world is not gradually cooling from a ball of fire, but has gradually grown in size by absorb- ing other smaller masses of matter. The earth- quake is really only a trivial phenomenon of the earth. The great question for us, he argues, is no,. what disasters impend, but what agencies are likely to perpetuate life. The human race really has just come into possession of the earth. The fact that the rocks and the animals have had their eras of prosperity is the basis for his belief that we have millions of years before us.

===== 0002601_19060908_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
CONTROLLING FLOWER COLOURS.

It is generally known that the colours of vegetation vary in intensity in a direct ratio to the amount of sunlight combined with coolness of temperature, within certain limits. Examples are the intense redness of apples grown in north- ern climates, and the deep colours of Alpine vegetation. But the soil, and other influences, also have an effect upon plant colours. Mr. Henry Kriemer has experimented upon the arti.' ficial control of the colours of plants through he introduction of chemicals into the soil they grow in. In very small quantities such chemicals are absorbed without apparent injury, but the et bet upon the colours is slight. "Yellow roses, for in- stance, appeared to become deeper in cobur under the influence of aluminium sulphate and potassium sulphate. With the use of these same chemicals the petals of the white .carnation shewed a tendency to develop red streaks, where- as when fed with ammonium sulphate, alumi- nium sulphate, iron citrate; and citrio acid, scar. let carnations tended to form white streaks.

HOW THE TELEPHONE WAS BORN.

This momentous invention was, as so many other great inventions have been, the result of experiments made in connection with something I quite different. Professor Bell, whilst Professor of Vocal Physiology in ,Boston University, turned his attention to multiplex telegraphy. One day, we are told, a wira snapping in two sent a sound through another wire which had attached to each end a thin sheet-iron disc ti few inches in circumference. Could that sound be repeated? Experiment gave an affirmative answer. Then arose the important query, "Could vocal sounds be transmitted thus?" A parchment diaphragm with it sheet-iron button in the centre was stretched across the mouth of a thin metal cylinder about 3in. in diameter. A glimpse inside that metal tube would .have shewn us features not unknown in to-day's perfected re- ceiver—two magnets with poles wound with wire and, between the magnets, a small strip of soft iron. A precisely similar instrument, with a wire running from its coils, was left in charge of Bell's assistant, while Bell, with the wire con- nected with his tubular iron-cased telephone, ascended to the attic of his house. The assist- ant, a briglit young man, was directed to remain in the laboratory and to keep the receiver at his ear. Bell, holding the diaphragm a few inches from his lips, said in ordinary conversational tones, "Can you hear me?" In a moment the assistant came bounding up the stairs. "Mr. Bell," he excitedly called out, "I heard your question plainly I"

ALUMINIUM-CONTAINING PLANTS.

Aluminium, says a writer in the Scientific American, is the radical of clay, for what the chemist designates as clay is merely a combina- tion of aluminium and oxygen in certain pro- portions. Now since clay (as the name already indicates) is contained in all argillaceous kinds of soil, we may at once conclude that this element has a vast distribution in the superficial strata of the earth. Hence it would be wholly inex- plicable if plants were to take up from the soil no aluminium whatever, or only in exceptional cases. Hitherto o however, only a very few vege- table growths have been known in which aluminium can be shown; especially, snake-moss (Lycopodiuta). To clear up this discrepancy, the University of Odessa appointed a competition in which further investigations on the presence of aluminium in plants were to be conducted. Two young Russian botanists who devoted themselves to the task have reached the result that all the plants investigated by them take up aluminium in greater or lees quantity when it is made ac- oeesible to them in proper form, and that, too, not only from the soluble but also item some salts insoluble in water, as from clay phosphates. That aluminium has been so seldom found in plants is to be explained by the fact that it is retained mostly or wholly in the roots. Above all, however, it is to bo considered that alu- minium, in spite of its general distribuf ion, is but seldom contained in such combinations in the soil as can be, worked up by the plants. That is an advantage, indeed, because the soluble salts of the clay even when greatly diluted exert a poisonous effect upon plants, especially upon the roots when the clay is touched while they are still in rapid growth. Yet—as is indeed very often the case also with the effect of poisons upon the human and animal organisms—small quantities of aluminium may be absolutely help- ful in the development of the plant. ,

PRUSSIC ACID IN PLANTS.

In the quarterly scientific journal "Scientific Progress in the Twentieth Century" there is an interesting summary of the latest discoveries in oonnection with the occurrence of prussic acid and its derivatives in plants. One of the most striking points, perhaps, is the wide-spread oc- currence of this vegetable poison. It has been found in plants belonging to no fewer than twenty-two natural orders, including the grasses, lilies, willows, flaxes, &c: In 1902 a list of over 100 species of plants containing prussic acid was compiled by Jouck, and this number has since been added to. . The most familiar occurrence of prussic acid is in the oil of bitter almonds. The occurrence of prussic acid has been proved in many plants of economic importance, It is. for example, the bitter principle in the% roots of the Cassava plants grown in the tropics, from which the tapioca of commerce is prepared. Prussic acid also has been shewn to occur in the roots of the plant yielding the Ceara rubber of commerce, and in the seeds, leaves, and flowers of that yielding Para rubber. Several species of vetch contain it in their seeds. Prussic acid, again, has been discovered in the young green parts of the common sorghum, or great millet, a plant widely cultivated as a grain which forms a staple foodstuff in 'lndia, Egypt, and other countries. ThA leaves of maize have also been shewn to contain it. And an annual leguminous plant, a species of lotus, growing along the valley of the Nile, is poisonous in the earlier stages of its growth, though forming a u,seful fodder when mature. Another species of lotus growing in Australia has obtained notoriety as a .cattle poisoner in a similar way. Investigations shew that the prussic acid does not occur in plants irs the free state, but in com- bination, forming what is termed a glucoside. Under the action of a suitable ferment this latter splits up into glucose, prussic acid, and another compound varying according to the particular glucoside. In the bitter almond the glucoside is known as amygdalin, which under the action of the ferment •emulsion yields prussic acid and a benzene derivative, which together form oil of bitter almonds. The lotus contains the glucoside I lotusin and the ferment lotase.

The function of prussic acid in the physiology of the plant has been much discusseof. Earlier tnvestigators suggested that prussic acid in a plant was a means of protection. A later view was that it was merely a waste product of meta- bolism stored up.* in bark, leaves, &c. Now, however, prussic acid is thought to play an im- portant role in the life of the plant. Treub, for example, as the result of elaborate investigations, concludes that the production of this substance is the tu-st stage in the assimilation Ot nitrogen from the nitrates in the soil. Prussic acid ia,a derivative of cyanogen, and to this substance and its derivatives Gautier and others assign the chief place in the building up of plant proteida
