IN A TOWN GARDEN.

The following verses will probably be read with interest on the occasion of the Beautiful Warrington Society's First Annual Flower Show. They were written by Alderman Bennett about a year ago, during his residence in Salisbury-street, and originally published in "The Dawn" The crocus army lifts its spears To guard my plot of garden ground ; The tulip its bright lamp uprears, Regardless of the pall profound; While timidly the snowdrop pale Peeps at me with her tiny face— Alas, the damsel is too frail To flourish in this smoky place ! But soon the hyacinths unclose Their vials of such scents as rise Where the sweet Crystal River flows Through the far fields of Paradise; And, bolder yet, the daffodils Stand with their hanging sheaves of gold, While March his breezy trumpet shrills, And sparrows shiver in the cold. And, ere the last keen frosts are gone, The almonds brings its milky blooms, Before its soft leaves seek the sun— The smoke their brief existence dooms, But, hardier, the wild apple wears Its bridal garment, white as snow, And, for the tenth time, bravely dares The rigours of its sooty foe. The guardian poplars, feebler grown, Still make their sturdy annual fight; The gilded laurel struggles on, And lifts its armour to the light; The elders loose their golden leaves, The privet dons its emerald mail, And lovingly the ivy weaves Its clinging robe, which shall not fail. The beech puts forth such living green As all the chimneys shall not stain, And stands, its sister trees between, Washed greener by the silver rain ; The birch, a mighty maiden-hair, Waves dainty arms above the grass Towards the blue clematis there— . I count twelve blossoms as I pass ! And, in the smoky dragon's teeth, The soft Virginian creeper crawls My windows lovingly beneath, And makes a glory of the walls, And climbs and creeps, and will not rest Until it clasps the grateful eaves, While, night by night, the golden west Lays liquid gold on ruby leaves. Blue-eyed, the wee lobelias weep, Exiles from some far Fairyland ; Blue-eyed nemophilas sadly peep At two blue eyes that understand ; Hard-by, the corn-flowers, crowned each one With stainless purple, each a king, Reproach their courtier, the sun, The birds, which all two seldom sing. How bright the brave nasturtiums are, And who shall stay the marigold ? Its every bloom a yellow star With clustering petals manifold ; While, in the mimic firmament, . Reflecting the great heavenly flame, The shining sun-flowers smile content, And kinship with their author claim. Carnations bare their crimson hearts, Though hideous earwigs hide them there, And, close at hand, a lily starts To find herself so strangely fair, And hides beneath a sheltering tree To screen her whiteness from the gaze Of winds that watch her lustfully, And the warm sunlight's amorous rays. The dahlias tall reach out to me, And pay their homage as I pass: Where can the laggard asters be ? What honey-bowls the poppy has! And, in the shadowy corner high, A hundred emerald buds still hold, Arrone by one the weeks slip by, The yellow daisies, suns of gold. But lo ! the autumn in the air, The morning diamonds on the grass ; There comes an end to all things fair; My flowers will fade, and I must pass To ampler gardens, and a sky Less clouded, but not cease to love The garden where ten years have I Watched the eternal pageant move. From grim smoke-dragons sought to guard These fairies, and their fangs to stay, And from my struggling stretch of sward Turned to the splendid spire to pray ; And often, in the smoky town. Perceived some trace of Eden yet, Dawn's magic splendour stealing down To cancel grimy Mammon's debt. And flowers in scores, and leaves untold, And birds whose music naught could dumb, And morning glory and sunset gold, Have often aureoled my home, And taught me still, from day to day, The giant chimneys just beneath, How beauty helds continual sway Right in the very jaws of Daath,

PATIENTS 'TERRIBLE JUMP.

An inquest held at Westminster on Wednesday to investigate the death of Charles G. W. Griffiths, a New Malden gas fitter, elicited the fact that he jumped from a third storey window of Charing-cross Hospital. He had been under an operation, successfully performed, and whilst the nurse's back was turned he opened the window and jumped into the street. The hcspital house surgeon said deceased's mind might have been affected by the operation, or the heat. A verdict of " Suicide whilst of unsound mind "was returned.

M GOOD FAIRY

RACHEL.

Your loving Nephew,

•

Melba, the Queen of the Fairies, was despair. ing of ever getting her subjects to love one another. There was Karel, she had that day let a little boy steal some appl,s,lbaud then put the thcrught into his mother's head to chide him. She ought to have whispered in the boy's ear, telling him that it was wrong to steal, and then, perhaps, he Might not have stolen them. Queen Melba watched all her fairies, and found them all the same, except one, "Rachel" by name. Rachel went off early every morning, before the other fairies were awake, and nothing would be heard of her till night, when she would come home, and go straight to sleep in a foxglove. Melba, being curious to know what Rachel did, got up early one morning, and, taking the form of a bee, followed her. When she had followed -Rachel to a small alley, she hid, and watched. Rachel -first went into a poor house and, taking the form of a nurse, i helped a. poor woman in all k de of coarse it. work. The poor woman loved R hel, as could well be seen, and as she went aw y, the woman said, "God blete you, Rachel." Rachel went. from house to house in this manner, helping the poor, and they one and all loved her. In the afternoon, Rachel took an invisible form to the human eye, although a fellow-fairy could see her. Then she went to where poor beggars sat in despair, and she whispered words of comfort, and when a gentleman passed by she would speak Words of pity in his ear, and he would throw them a coin. In the evening, Rachel went to all the little children, and liushed them to sleep, so that they would not wake their Mothers. Then Rachel, worn out with hard work, made her way home, and went silently to bed in her foxglove. Queen Melba had now seen the only good fairy she had, a-nd so that night, when Rachel was asleep, she ordered all her fairies to follow Rachel in un- suspicious forms, so that they might learn how to love. They followed her next morning, but, on the way, Rachel saw a poor boy drowning. Now, in those. days, if a fairy got wet, she would live but a few hours afterwards. Rachel, knowing this, did not hesitate, but went into the water and saved the poor lad, and, coming out, in front of her stood Melba and all the other fairies. They stood by Rachel, and all kissed her before he died, and then they all dressed, in white (which is the fairies' form of maurning), and buried her in a bed of lilies. Next day all the fairies went to work, but not as they usually did, for, although her life had been the forleit, Rachel had taught the fairies a lesson.of love. The other fairies have not for- gotten Rachel, although they wear various colours, all but Queen Melba, who always wears white in memory of the Good Fairy Rachel.

FITZROY LOVELL.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am sending you a letter to, thank you for that beautiful knife you sent me. I think I am going to have my name put on it. My sister is sending a letter and a poet-card, and I am also sending you a letter and a post- card, which I hope you like. We will soon be beginning sohool, and have only another week's holiday. I have not used the penknife yet, but I like it very much. We have begun harvest, and I like to help them. I was helping them yesterday to. cut wheat. I think I have no mots to say this time, so I will stop.

, GEORGE ERNEST COATES.

OPPORTUNITY SPEAKS. Yes, I am Opportunity; But say, young man, don't wait for me °P.. To come to you; Just buckle down to win your crown, And work with head And heart and hands, As does the man who understands That those who wait, Expecting some reward from fate, Or luck—to call it so— Sit always in the 'way-back row. I serve the active mind, The seeing eye, The ready hand, that grasps me passing by, And takes from me the good I hold, For every spirit, strong and bold. Lie does not wait on fate Who seizes me, For I am fortune, luck and fate—: The corner-stone of what is great In man's accomplishment. But I am none of these To him who does not seize; I must be caught, If any good is wrought Out of the treasures I possess. Oh, yes; I'm Opportunity; I'm great; I'm sometimes late, But do not wait for me; Work off, watch on; Good hands, good heart, And some day you will see Out of your efforts rising— Opportunity! .

i‘x DEAR CHILDREN,-

I have been very pleased with the large number of Picture Postcards sent me during the last few days. It really has been difficult to decide whioh, are the prettiest. I must here mention that postcards were sent me without any address, and however much I wished to award a prize for same, I could not do so. Also, during the week some of my members have sent cards asking for a bottle of Mason's Extract of 1:1 erbos. As several of these applicants sent no address, I have been forced to told them over until hearing further from them.

I have sent prizes to Ernest Harrold and Fitzroy Lovell, for their contributions, which I consider most deserving. As regards the Postcard Competition, I have decided to give the prizes to N. Andrews for her "Kitten" postcard; to Nellie Roberts (her card was a pretty country scene); and to G. E. Coates, for his comic card. The first two names mentioned sent no address, so until I receive same I must keep back the prizes. I am sure you will all like the tale sent me by H. Dux. bury. I hope next time this member sends a contribution I shall be able to send him a prize. With all good wishes from

tircLE N.

Dear Uncle Ned,—lt gives me pleasure to write you a few lines about the way I spent my holiday?. I only received one day; but I an sorry to say I was very ill on that ind the day following. But on Saturday I had a day off, and now I will tell you how—

" I SPENT MY HOLIDAY AT STOURPORT AND 'WORCESTER."

On Saturday morning I started from home for a day's trip to Stourport and Woreester,l which I thoroughly enjoyed. The train started from Dudley ° Station at nine o'clock, and arrived at Kidderminster at 9.45. We then went to Stourport by tram, and it was a very breezy wind which made the weather very pleasant. We arrived at Stourport, and went down by the river; where there were many people enjoying themselves in rowing boats. We hired one for an hour or, so, and rowed about two miles up the river. There were some splendid sights to be seen, viz. youths swimming, boys bathing, and children paddling, with their shoes and stockings off. The. cows were also in the river, up to their knees in the water; and when a steam- boat would come past and cleave the water, the cows would rush out as hard as they could, till the boat was far past them. When we returned the boat to the waterman, we were all ready to start on our journey to Worcester by a steamboat, called the Amo. The boat went very fast, and we arrived at Worcester in about two Lours and a-half. We passed that beautiful bridge at Holt Fleet, and, further up the river, there were many people camping on the riverside, and there were also many house-boats on the river. We arrived at Worcetiter, and had tea, and then went straight to the Cathedral. It is a beautiful place inside, And we visited all the tablets •and monuments before the, service began. We had only an, hour to stop at Worcester, and when we came out of the Cathedral, we had to go straight to the 'boat, and return to Stourport, from whence we went to Kidder- minster, and visited Rowland Hill's statue. We then continued our way to the station, and arrived at Dudley at eleven o'clock. So we had a good day's outing.

I remain, your loving Nephew, ERNEST HARROLD.

Augur of mischief, pipes for rain— Bob White! Men who reap on the fruitful plain

Warrington v. Leigh.

As in 1905 Warrington on Saturday opened their season by a match with Leigh but were unable to repeat last year's victory, the gama ending in a draw. The meeting attracted some 5,000 spectators to the Leigh ground and Leigh started against the breeze but with the sun at their backs. Harmer returned O'Neill's kick and the exchanges which followed resulted in favour of Leigh. In the first few minutes it appeared as though the home team would more, as Berry was collared before he could clear. Taylor however came to the rescue and saved splendidly. Immediately afterwards Leigh were given a free kick but Burgess's attempt at goal was a miserable failure. A rush by the Warrington forwards was well stopped by Clarkson, and then passing by Bennett, Whitehead and Neville removed play to the Warrington end. After a lot of exchange kicking the Warrington backs started passing, and Brooks was brought down near the line. S. Johnson made a run through the Warrington team, but failed to pass Berry. Lees broke away splendidly' and the ball travelled in the direction of Taylor who however kicked a little too hard. After Leigh had I missed a chance the Warrington forwards dribbled to the Leigh end, and Taylor got over but tha point was I not allowed. At the interval there was no score. On I resuming, Warrington attacked vigorougly, and Brooks scored as the result of a fine passing movement, no goal resulting. Some kicking exchanges laded in the ball bouncing to Brooks who bad a clear field, and he ran in from half-way. Fish placed a goal. Tom Johnson scored for Leigh, the place kick failing. Shortly after- wards Whittakbr scored under the posts, O'Neill gosling. Result :—Leigh 1 goal, 11 tries (8 points), Warrington 1 goal, 2 tries (8 points). Teams : Leigh—E. Clarkson, full back ; S. Johnson, J. ET. Johnson, IC Bennett (captain), and K. H. Neville,three- quarter backs ; S. Whittaker and A. Burgess, half backs; P. O'Neill, A. Lee, N. Webster, W. Winstanley, jun., J. A. Blackburn and R. Berry, forwards. Warrington—Berry, full back ; Fish (captain), Isher- wood, Taylor, and Brooks, three-quarter backs ; Hocken- hall and Lees, half backs ; Pieston, Thomas, Naylor, Harmer, Heath, and Boardman, forwards. Referee, Mr. H. Edmundson (Bradford) ; touch judges, Mr. Jones (Runcorn) and Mr. Edwards (Broughton).

===== 0002601_19060908_art0187.txt | included=true | included =====
OLDHAM v. RTJNCORN.

Flayed at Watersheddings, before a moderate attend- ance. Ferguson kicked off, and Oldham pressed for a time. After fifteen minutes play Dixon andgash rushed the ball down the fleld,Dixon:scoring. No go.il followed. Richardson kicked a penalty goal, and Watton scored cleverly before half-time, Jolley adding a goal, EO that at the interval Runcorn led by seven points to three. This lead the visitors kept until three-quarter time, then Owens scored close td the line, and White kicked a line goal. Jolley kicked a penalty goal, putting his team again ahead. Splendid passing ended in Butter- worth scoring a try. Jolley missed the goal. Result : Runcorn 3 goals, / tries (12 points), Oldham 1 goal, 2 tries (8 points).

WIGAN v. BARROW. Barrow were the visitors at the opening of the season at Central Park, Wigan, where some 8,000 persons assembled. Barrow were pressed early on, and within the first fire or six minutes Leytham raced over their line, and landed a fine goal from touch. Lowe followed with a sFaartly registered try, and prior to the interval Leytham had again crossed. Mason kicked the goal, whilst Brooke added a fourth try, the score at the interval being in favour of the home lot by 16 points to nothing. For a considerable period in the second half the home team lacked the services of Mason. who hid been overcome by the sun's rays' but notwithstand- ing this the, 'Wiganers completely outplayed their opponents, whose line was repeatedly crossed. Result : —Wigan, 9 goals and 12 tries (54 points) ; Barrow, nil.

===== 0002601_19060908_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. HELENS v. SWINTON.

Played at St. Helens, before a good gate. St. Helens at once gained a footing in the Swinton half. The visitors attempted to pass out, but Valentine mulled a nice opening. Bocook, the Saints' new half back, opened the game out and set his forwards in possession. Swinton entered the home half, and from a kick by Dixon the visitors got away with a rush. From a cross- Mick Lloyd was enabled to score a try, Wallwork improving. Toole got away, and after a grand run, passed to Briers, who scored. The kick at goal failed. Half-time score: Swinton 1 goal and 1 try (5 points), St. Helens 1 try (3 points). Swinton got away in the loose, and Wallwork succeeded in snoring under the posts. Wallwork also improved the try. In the last minute Pope scored at the corner, Smith failing at the goal kick. Result :—Swinton 2 goals and 2 tries (10 points), St. Helens 2 tries (0 points).

FRIENDLY MATCH.

WARRINGTON "A" v. WIDNES RANCERS.—A moder- ate attendance assembled to witness the opening match of the season on the Wilderspool enclosure. There was one alteration from the team originally selected, Kay taking the place of Brooks at half-back. The Rangers opened strongly, making an excursion to the Warrington end in the first minute. Knight saved nicely,however, and a fine passing bout between Davies, Dickenson and Lander put the " Wirepullers " on a more equal footing. Immediately afterwards some fine work on the left wing culminated in Cartwright scor- ing a try from a pass by Davies, the goal points not being added. Warrington infringed the off-side rule,and Noon scored the Ranger's first try, Dale adding the goal. Encouraged by their success, Widnes again attacked, but Warrington were sturdy in their defence. Davies secured the ball, but his efforts were neutralized by the Rangers' failing with the goal. Kay quickly added another try, Foster putting on the extra points. Interval : Warrington, one goal four tries (14 points); Widnes Rangers one goal one try (five points). War- rington practically had the game in their own hands in the second half,Landor, Jolley, and Foster all scoring. The game ended in Warrington's favour by 36 points to 5.

Warrington Rural District Council. The monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council was held at Bewsey Chambers on Wednesday when there were present Messrs. W. Houghton (presiding), and G. Mather, J. Thompson,J. W. Knowles, J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk) B. S. Newcomb (surveyor), H. H. Burnage assistint surveyor), and F. W. Kerfoot (inspector) and Dr. Sephton, (medical officer). The Clerk recom- mended the levying of a call of sd. in the X, as against 4d. for the past half year, and calls for special expenses in the various districts Penketh £l4o,Poulton S..so,.Rixton £4O, Great Sankey £3O, and Burtonwood £l5O. The Medical Officer reported that during the month there had been 24 births and 28 deaths. The death rate was equal to 28 per 1,000 as against 28, the average of the past three months and 33.5 for the corresponding period of last year. Seven of the deaths occui red at the County Asylum, Winvvick, exclusive of which the death rate proper for the dis- ict was equal to 21 per 1,000. Messrs. S. and F. Acton rem given the contract for poiliting and repair- ing guide posts in he district for £l5 Os.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

A SINGULAR EXCUSE.

lOn Monday at the Warrington Occasional Court, before Mr. F. Monks, Arthur Ford, 2, York-street, 1 Latchford, was charged with having been drunk on 1 Saturday night, and he and Thomas Henry Dunbobin 48, New-road, Latchford, were charged with stealing at 11.30 p.m. on Saturday, 131 b ofiples, valued 2s. 2d., m from the orchard at Lub Bro Cottage, Latchford Without, the property of Harri Warburton.—Mr. Steel who appeared for the defendants,explained that on Satur- day they hired a horse and trap to drive to the football match at Leigh, and at Leighthey had too much drink. When they were returning at night they had occasion to alight from the trap in Acker's-lane, and then in theft drunken state went into the orchard and took the apples. Their action could not be justified in any way because the apples were not fit to eat, and their taking the fruit could only be accounted for by the fact that they were under the influence of drink. It was their first oftence.—Ford was fined ss. for having been drunk and for the theft Ford and Dunbobin were each fined 15s. _ HUSBAND'S HABITUAL DRUNKENNESS.

- -- At the Borough Police Court on Saturday, before Mr. R. T. Fairclough (presiding), Alderman Tinnion and Mr. A. J. Jolley, Charles Littlewood, 28, Battersby- lane, was charged on a warrant to show cause why a separation order should not be granted to his wife on the ground that he was an habitual drunkard. Prisoner admitted that he was occasionally drunk.—Complainant stated that it was her husband's habit to come home I trunk twice a day. He returned last Saturday with his money, and in answer to her question he said he had nothing for her. He went out immediately, and came home drunk at night. On Sunday he was also drunk, and after having had a sleep, went out, and returned drunk in the evening. On Monday he was drunk twice. On Monday night she objected to his coming into the house, which was in her name, and a constable came to her assistance. There were nine children, the youngest being nine years of age. He had not worked regularly for three years until last week. She asked for a separ- ation order.—Prisoner said he had been in hospital for a year and eight months, and he was still suffering from a diseased shoulder, with the result that he could not follow his employment —A separation order was granted, and prisoner was ordered to pay his wife ss. a week. _

I On Monday at the Warrington Police Court; Joseph Barlow, 45, New-road, was charged on a warrant with out having answered his summons, to show cause why his wife should not be granted a separation order on the gronnds of his persistent cruelty and his being an habitual drunkard.—Complainant said her husband had only worked four weeks this year. She had had to work to keep the house open. Ile was continually drunk and be had treated her scandalously, using bad language and threatening to " rip her up." Whilst she was in bed ill he had illtreated her. On one occasion he tried to make her swallow twopence. Every penny .he got hold of he 'spent in drink. She had frequently been obliged to take refuge in a neighbour's houses; but the factory had been her only shelter.—Mrs. Isherwood, a neighbour rave evidenoe in support.— Inspector Cox, of the N.S.P.C.C., deposed to having known prisoner since September, 1905. Since then he had been continually drinking, with the exception of three months when he was teetotal.—A separation order with 10s. per week alimony was made.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must in an cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

The Payment of Mayor's Auditor. Sir,—lt having been ascertained this morning that Councillor Wilson has accepted payment as Mayor's auditor, it has been decided to take steps to test the legality of the payment, and the necessary proceedings against Councillor Wilson will be at once instituted. The Town Clerk has " not the slightest doubt" that the payment is legal ; the Municipal Corporations Act clearly enacts that no member of a Council shall, on any pretext, make a profit out of his membership of a Council. It is time we had the decision of the judges on the point. Of course Mr. Wilson is by his action, morally, if not legally, disqualified to sit in the Council, and whatever the legal decision may be, the Latchford ratepayers will be asked to give a verdict on the moral issue on the Ist November next.—Yours JAMES DODDS. September 7th. 190 G.

===== 0002601_19060908_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
The Condition of the Masses.

Sir,—Mr. W. Furness's letter is good reading and I hope it will make some of your readers think more about their duties as voters and responsible citizens. We want more of such letters and such efforts. If working men would take a tenth part as much interest in these matters as they do in football and horse racing their condition would improve rapidly. If a thousand books were written about Social Problems, one fact and One idea would overshadow everything else i.e. the workers, the oppressed, the sufferers, and the grumblers have the remedy in their own hands. A man has no moral right to complain about his condition if he will make no effort to improve it. Mr. Furness refers to the narrow methods and haphazard way in which local representa- tives are selected and I agree with the genenal tenour of his remarks, but I am not sure whether his suggested remedy about a large and representative public meeting will bear a close examination. How could we depend upon it being large and representative? Who would have sufficient authority to call such a meeting and assuming it was called and held, would not the sugges- tion and proposing of names be haphazard very often? Would it not be better to begin with a definite and positive programme? Having in my early career done a good deal of lecturing and public speaking I can assure Mr. Furness that public meetings are not in many cases either reliable or manageable nor do they always reflect public opinion fairly. There is another point to be considered. If a public meeting is not engineered in the interests of a particular candidate and the audience is relied upon to propose names, such names would in most cases include : 1, Men who are suitable, but not willing ; 2, Men who are willing but not suitable; 3, Men who are both willing and suitable. I need not say that this would arouse antagonistic feelings and end in confusion. Let us clear the ground first by getting rid of false pride and out of date notions of propriety. The preva- lent idea that a man should wait to be asked before offering himself as a candidate is elite out of place in the twentieth century. It has caused an enormous loss of energy and ability to every community and to the country. Every man is responsible to society in pro- portion to his abilities and opportunities and it should be considered a duty to the public to offer himself when he feels confident of his power to do good.As soon as this duty is fully and generally acknowledged our difficulties in the selection of candidates and in other . .

matters will almost disappear. My suggestion is, that every man as soon as he decides to be a candidate for I the Town Council or any local governing body, should inform the local papers, and at the same time send a brief statement of his views on the most important subjects. That a list of candidates be kept at the office of each local paper to be published whenever it iS desirable, and more especially when vacancies occur. As soon as a candidate's name is on the list he can take any' steps he may think fit to make himself and his opinions known to the voters, and when a vacancy occurs, there is on open field and a fair start for each. It will be admitted I think that this plan has at least the merit of straightfordwardness, which cannot be said for the present wirepulling method of class, clique or party, with their bar parlour conferences and imagin- ary deputations. It is hardly necessary to add that such a plan as this would make it almost impossible for an unknown man to be pitchforked into a ward by a few personal friends—a thing notuncommon in Warrington. Perhaps your readers will express their view about it: Yours W. C.

Right to Work Committee.

Sir,—With reference to the letter appearing in your issue of Saturday last from the Secretary of the "Right to Work "Committee, it will be noted that this Com- mittee sets itself up as an authority on the Unemployed question and censures the present Liberal Government for granting (pending the report of the Royal Com- mission on the Poor Laws) no more than £200,000 to assist the working of the Unemployed Workmen's Act in alleviating the distress during the coming winter. It would perhaps be well that the public and especially the working class section of it should clearly under- stand what this Committee it. The "Riga to Work" Committee was constituted early in the year at a meeting of what purported to be representatives of various trade unions in the town. The meeting nomin- ated three organizations—the Independent Labour Party, Trades Council and Labour Representation Committee, —to each appoint five delegates who would con- stitute the Committee. The trades unions them- selves, after hoisting this new-fancied Committee into the political atmosphere, disappear from the brisiness leaving the Committee, disconnected from its base, floating, without any sort of control from themselves—a course quite at variance with the democratic principles guiding true trade- unionism, and which seems to ca/1 in question the bona- fides of the Unions concerned. It will at once he sur- mised that any other organizations than the three named would find a difficulty in gaining admission to the Committee, seeing that the constituting Authority had tone; and this we have proved to be the case for the Progressive Labour League, which represents up,.vards of 700 working men, being naturally in full sympathy with the ostensible object of the Committee, and pre- suming that, if sincere, it would welcome the co-opera- tion of such an organization, appointed five delegates to represent our League on the Committee, and com- municated the fact to the conve,ner of the first meeting (Councillor Stott) asking to 13e notified of the same. We now learn that this startled Councillor Stott, which I can assure him was quite unintentional, but be replied thanking me for my letter, and pointing out that he would be exceeding his instructions if he summoned any other organization than the three named, but that the matter would be laid before the llrstrneeting. This meeting was held in due course and on May 19th Mr. Flampson, the secretary,wrote me as follows : "Your letter of April 21st was duly placed before the first meeting of above Committee, but it was felt that the Committee had hardly the right to invite delegates from other organizations save those decided upon at a s Tz;n4.,+ re, • - .enc, ',slid some time ago. The Examiner, Saturday, September 8, 1906.

NORTHWICH LIGHT RAILWAY. TRANIWAYS TO STRETTON.

Alderman SMETFIURST said he had been asked to mention the matter of the Northwich Light Railway. The position at present was that he Council were waiting for a reply to their letter with regard to the extension of the tramways to Stretton. Councillor WILKINSON thought the Corporation had spent sufficient money upon the tramways without carrying them to Stretton. They should let somebody else join with the lines to Warrington. Alderman SMETIICIIST said that was intended.

Councillor LORD : Can we have the Standing Order read? The MAYOR : No. The minutes were then approved. FIRE ENGINE COMMITTEE. APPLIANCES AT COCKHEDGE MILLS.

Letters having been read at the Fire Engine Com- mittee from Messrs. Armitage and Rigby stating that there was a shortage of water in case of fire at the Cockhedge Mills the committee resolved that in accord- ance with the above request the Corporation extend the main from the top of Crown-street to the entrance of the mills, and that the Borough Surveyor see Messrs. Armitage and Rigby Ltd. with a view to their making internal arrangements for fire protection. Alderman SNIETHURST said he thought the simplest remedy for the shortage of water would be for Messrs. Armitage and Rigby to run two twelve inch mains from their reservoir into a tank which could be constructed in the mill yard. Councillor MAGINN pointed out that Messrs. Walker's paid the cost of carrying the main from Winwick-street to their premises. Councillor WRIGHT thought that Messrs. Armitage and Rigby had placed themselves in a difficulty by covering the reservoir and wanted the Fire Engine Committee to get them out of it. He thought however that it was only reasonable that the Corporation main should be extended to the factory gate. The minutes were opproved. MARKETS COMMITTEE.

The Markets Committee had resolved, that the under- ground conveniences and cellar be ventilated in accord- ance with the tender of Messrs. Kieth and Blackman. In answer to Councillor Pemberton Councillor EVANS said he could not produce the tender as it was locked up in the Borough Surveyor's safe. The amount how- ever was under £2O. He would withdraw the minute.

Councillor SToTT : Has anything been done with regard to giving the contrast to the firm? Councillor EVANS : I don't think so.

Councillor PEMBERTON : I hope there has not because I want to see the tender. THE WATERWORKS CONTRACT. MR. LAWSON'S AMENDED BILL. At a meeting of the Water Committee on 27th August a letter was read from Mr. Lawson's solicitors forward- ing an amended claim against the Corporation in respect of the works at Delph-lane, and giving notice of their intention to apply to the County Court Judge for the appointment of an arbitrator. It was resolved, that in order to enable the claim to be examined Mr. Lawson's solicitors be requested to allow the application to the County Court Judge to lie over for six weeks. Alderman EvaNs moved and Alderman SMETHURST seconded, the confirmation of the minutes.

Alderman BENNETT asked when the report with regard to Lawson's contract was going to be produced. Councillor STOTT : Is it likely we shall have it before the six weeks ? Alderman EVANS : I cannot give you any informa- tion on that.

---- --- The Mason : It belongs to the General Purposes Committee.

Councillor LORD : I should like the Council to be put in possession of the amount of the claim, and the further claim which Mr. Lawson has sent in, I think the Council are entitled to know something about it. Alderman EvANS : I have not the figures exactly,but can give them in round numbers. The original claim was £54,000 ; the amended claim was £72,000. After a short conversation with Mr. Deas the Alderman said— In March, 1905, the claim was £56,680 is. id. ; an amended claim has been sent in and it amounts to £72499 19s. 11d. Councillor LORD, in answer to a member, said that although the Investigation Committee had-ibeen sitting 12 months, the amended claim of Mr. Lawson had come in only a day or two. Whether it was a final claim or not he (Councillor Lord) did not know. (Laughter). The MAYOR said that if he was Mr. Lawson and thouaht he could get it he would put on another £20,000. The minutes were passed. HALF-PENNY FARES. ALLEGED ABUSE OF THE CONCESSION. LIVELY SCENE.

On August 14th the Electricity and Tramways Com- mittee discussed the question of workmen's fares, and it was resolved that the Council be recommended to rescind the resolution of rd July, relative thereto, and that the following resolution be substituted :—" That Id. return tickets be issued before S a.m., and that the return half remain available on any day during the same week between the hours of 5 and 7 p.m." Alderman SMETHURST moved the adoption of this. He pointed out that a good deal of trouble had arisen in respect to workmen's fares and they found it almost impossible to carry out the s'ystem. He had been look- ing over the names of people who had travelled as workmen, and he was sure the Council would be astma- i ished if they heard who they were. The tram conductors ought not to be placed in the position to say who were workmen. It did not matter who got the return tickets so long as they travelled before 8 a.m. 'The question was asked if the tickets were transfer- able, and Aldermln Smethurst replied in the negltive. The ATATOR : Unfortunately, you cannot tell who has taken out a ticket.

—er-otTn—ciflo-r—WrckuvsoN seconded .the adoption of the minutes. f Councillor EVANS asked if the return tickets could be used on a Saturdav between 12 and 2 as at present, The NlAvon said it was understood that would be carried out. The present proposition would benefit not only workmen' but shop assistants and clerks who received small wages ; in fact, the whole community who travelled before S a.m. The only difference to workmen would be that they would have two tickets instead of one. The halfpenny fare would disappear altogether. Councillor BALL moved an amendment that the matter be referred back for further consideration. He did not think the workmen's tickets hid-had a sufficient trial. Those people who had abused the privilege should be prosecuted, and A test made of the matter. People had insisted on having workmen's tickets who could well afford to pay the proper fare. It was quite true there MIS no regulation for Corporation tramways on this point. The majority of progressive Corporations had, however, the system of .(I.single fares for working men, however,and the concession was much appreciated. fie would not like to see it cast aside in Warrington. Councillor PEMBERTON seconded the amendment. He did not, however, agree that ratepayers---should be prosecuted because he believed PeoPlo who /were not workingmen had travelled for 3d. under the impression that it was right for them to do so. Alderman SMETHURST then withdrew the notion on the understanding that the matter would be again con- sidered in committee.

A SCENE. Councillor WRIGHT wished to say something on the fares question, and said he was on his feet before the resolution was withdrawn. He held in his hand two resolutions passed by the employees of SV. D. Honghton's wireworks and those of the Mersey White Lead Works, Sankey Bridges. The ifAvon It strikes me they are very selfish when they won't let others travel. Councillor WHIG HT : These are men who know what they want. They have a right to he considered. The :VIAYOR : This is not on the minutes. Alderman SMETHURST : The resolution will be brought before the committee. Councillor WRIGHT: Half a "mo."—Continuing he again urged that the workmen had a right to be : considered.

The MAYOR : Will you take my ruling ? Councillor WRIGHT : Yes, if you will allow me to have my say and don't interrupt me. I object to he continually interrupted. I was up before Alderman Smeth ii rat.

The MAYOR : You can behave yourself, can't you ? CcoinCillOr WRIGHT (shouting) : When I get behaviour I always give it back. If lam bullied I can bully es hard as anybody. The INTAYOR : Will you he a gentleman? Councillor WRIGHT: Yes, I can be a gentleman. I want the employees of these two firms to be considered.

LOCAL EXAMINATION RESULTS. DISTINCTIONS FOR WARRINGTON STUDENTS.

•

Below we publish the examination results of the Board of Education and the City and Guilds of London Institute held recently. They were submitted to the School Management Committee at the meeting on Thursday, and although the comparative statement has not yet been made, we believe they are better than last year. We include for the first time the Acting Teacher's examinations results, in which Mr. Dendy, Mr. Turner, and Miss Ewart obtained distinctions. Two bronze medals have been awarded by the City and Guilds of London Institute to Mr. Joseph Owen for brickwork and Mr. Quentin Walter Wallington for leather tanning, whilst Messrs. Dale and Massey's work in carpentry And joinery, for the examination, was of such excellence that the City and Guilds have borrowed it for the purpose of exhibiting it at an exhibition at Belfast. We have already referred to Miss Mottram, who is to 'be congratulated on obtaining one of the two national scholarships in art. Miss Pearson, one of the art pupil teachers, had her work in geometrical drawing accepted for the art master's certificate. All the schools re-open on Monday evening and the results set out below will be a stimulus to the students to renew their efforts so that the result of the next year's examinations will be better still.

ACTING TEACHERS CERTIFICATE EXAMINATION, 1901 Pass: Emily Bowles, Rose Craik, Charles B. Dendy (distinction in education), Annie M. Ewart (distinction in mathematics and French), Ada M. Newbold, Florence Sutton, William Turner (distinction in English).

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

COMPENSATION FOR LICENCES.

DECISION NOT YET ARRIVED AT.

Big Claims at Warrington.

The Warrington Compensation Authority held a meeting at the Police Court on Thursday to assess the amount of compensation to be paid in respect of three licences which have been extinguished. The magistrates present were Mr. John Fairclough (in the chair), Aldermen Francomb, Bennett and Tinnion, Councillor Wilkinson, Messrs. C. White, T. Geddes, J. W. Hayward, H. Roberts, C. Jackson Holmes, W. Richardson, G. Pilling, R. T. Fairclough and S. Wright.

===== 0002601_19060908_art0208.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ROPE AND ANCHOR INN.

In the case of the Rope and Anchor Inn Mr. A. Browne appeared for the owners. Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co., and also for the tenant, Mr. Donworth. The total amount claimed he said was £1,86945.The Bench need not trouble themselves about the compensation to be awarded to the tenant as it had been agreed between Messrs. Greeinall, Whitley and Co. and Mr. Donworth that he should receive .Cl5O.

Mr. Roberts : We must approve of it. Mr. Browne : But when I say that we have agreed I am sure you will approve of the amonnt. Mr. Roberts : What the tenant is to receive is for 1 us to decide. Mr. Browne: Pardon me. Not if we agree. I will address the chairman. But it makes no difference to the claim. Continuing he said that for the past three 1 years an average of 151 barrels of beer had been sold la year at the Rope and Anchor. A profit of 14s. Gd. per barrel on that number meant £lO9 9s. 6d. Alderman Tinnion : Is the profit never a varying quantity ? Mr. Browne : 14s. Gd. is the av age for the past three years. The profit out of the bottl d trade averaged £lO 17s. 6d., making a total of £l2O s. An article in .1 the "Justice of the Peace " said on the question of ascertaining the compensation : "In a normal case find the average annual profits and multiply by twelve. To this add the capitalized value of the rent and a further sum for depreciation of trade fixture From this total deduct the capitalized rent likely to bes received when the house is no longer licensed and the result is the amount of the compensation." In making out their claim they had gone exactly on those lines. At present they received £3O rent and that capitalized at 20 years purchase made a total of £6OO. Deprecia- tion of fixtures accounted for £l2O and the alteration of the premises would cost £l9l. They were advised to alter the Inn into a shop. Mr. H. Roberts : That does not come within this case at all. 1

Mr. Browne claimed that the item had to be con- sidered. If the premises were not altered they would only, as a house, produce a rent of between £l2 and £l5 per annum. If the amount was allowed and the premises altered they would get £27 a year. The gross amount of the claim was therefore £2,355 4s. From this had to be deducted the capitalized rent of the shop at 18 years purchase, and when that was done they arrived at the amount of the claim which he had entered. Mr. Roberts thought there should not be the differ- ence in the number of years purchase of two years as between the rent as the inn and the rent of the shop. Mr. Browne : Yes. One is a licensed house and the other is not. A licensed house has more value, and is worth more years purchase than a shop. Councillor Wilkinson : Do you think you could let the shop if you spend £l9l on the alteration ? Mr. Browne : Yes, at once.

===== 0002601_19060908_art0209.txt | included=true | included =====
QUEEN'S HOTEL

In this application Mr. Holloway appeared on behalf of the Corporation, and Mr. J. Longland on behalf of Mr. Richard Sanson, the tenant. Mr. Holloway said that the amount of compensation claimed was £l,Bll 15s. The property was assessed for income tax, under schedule A, at £l4O 175., and the Corporation estimated that by expending £5OO on the conversion of the premises they would be able to get an income of £OO per annum. If the property was not altered they would get about £2O a year. The differ- ence between the two rents, capitalized at 23, years purchase meant £1,271 15s. To this had to beadded £5OO the cost of converting the premises, £2O for deprecia- tion and £2O for loss of rent during reconstruction of the premises, thus bringing the total to £l,Bll 13s. Mr. Browne : May lamend my claim. I have not included any loss of rent. (Laughter.) Mr. H. Roberts : I would like to ask whether the last £2O is a serious claim or whether it is a joke ? Mr. Holloway: It is perfectly serious. The Chairman : Is there any arrangement in respect to the tenant ?

- Mr. Hallaway : No. That is left to the Bench. Mr. Longland suggested that the tenant should receive betweed £3OO and £lOO. Mr. Browne was proceeding to question the applica- tion wkien Mr. Mr. Hallaway objected and contended that Messrs. Greenall Whitley and Co. had nothing to do with the case.

Mr. Browne : As the largest contributors to the compensation fund it affects our pocket. Mr. Longland pointed out that Mr. Sanson held the hotel on lease, two years of which had still to run. His rent was £125 a year. He had gone carefully into the figures as to the profits of the house, and found that they averagod £5 a week. He was entitled at least to £250, and to another half-year's profit of £125. Mr. Roberts : What have you charged in arriving at the net profit?

Mr. fengland : Everything. Rent, rates, gas, coal, servants, &c. Mr. Roberts : If my recollection serves me, at the original application it was stated that the house only just made a profit. Mr. Longhand : I said at that time that the takings were £2O to £2l a week for the whole of the time. Alderman Tinnion : Do you suggest that on a taking of .£2O a week you get a nett profit of 25.' per cent? Mr. Loogland : Ido in this case. This is a free house and the same amount is not charged for liquor as in the case of a tied house. Alderman Tinnion : it is a good business. THE BOAR'S HEAD.

• Mr. Knowles appeareed on behalf of Mrs. Fraser, proprietor of the Boar's Head Inn,tho tenant and Messrs, P. Walker and Sons being represented by Mr. T. S. Steel.

Mr. Knowles said that the total claim was for £2,334 13s. 6d., made up of £1,762 10d. claimed by Mrs. Fraser, £322 3s. 6d. by Messrs. P. Walker and Sons and £lOO by the tenant. Messrs. Wright, Garnett and Wright had drawn up Mrs. Fraser's claim and made it up as follows : Value of licensed house at twenty- live years' purchase £2,000 less value of cottages, into which it could be eonverted,at a nett rent of £7 Its. 6d. at 25 years' purchase after the cost of the conversion had been deduced £1,742 10s. This with £2O added for depreciation of fixtures brought the total to £1,762 10s. Messrs. Walkers only claimed three years' profits and I not twelve as suggested by Mr. Browne. Mr.G.F. F. White(magistrates' clerk) : They are not the actual owners and that makes the difference. Mr. Knowles continuing said that the average sales at the house were 2141 barrels a year, This at 13s. Gil a barrel gave £144 19s. 6d. Half-a-crown a gallon on the spirits sold brought the claim to £487 3s. 6d.and the addition of £35 for depreciation of fixtures made up the claim £522 3s. Gd. The Chairman ; Messrs. Walker had a lease on the premises ?

• Mr. Knowles : They are yearly tenants. They bad a lease whith terminated on the Ist May this year. They paid £BO a year for rent. Mr. G. F. Jr. White : I believe the house was sub- let and the tenant paid £4O a year to Messrs. Walker. Mr. Knowles explained that Messrs. Walker received £4O a year and 4s. a barrel which brought the total to over £SO a year. Mr. Steel submitted that the claim of the tenant for £lOO compensation was only reasonable. He was a man well advanced in years and was not able to follow any employment. Mr. H. Roberts : lam not satisfied as to the locus standi of Messrs. Walker's claim at all. Where does the interests of a yearly tenant come in ? Mr. Steel : I submit that Messrs. Walker need not have gone out until twelve months next May, and that being so they are entitled to some consideration. Mr. FI. Roberts : I would like to be shown that part of the Act which establishes the claim of your client. Mr. Steel : Walkers were the recognized tenants of Mrs. Fraser and section 2 undoubtedly applies to them. Mr. Roberts : But you cannot have two tenants. Does not your second client merge into a manager and as manager has he any claim ? Mr. Steel submitted that the licensee of the house was entitled to benefit by the legislation. After a brief retirement the Bench intimated that they did not approve of the amounts of the claims which they referred to the Inland Revenue Authorities. The question of compensation to the tenants must wait the decision of the Inland Revenue and until then the meeting stood adjourned. Mr. Browne : To save trouble, if any reasonabl3 offer is made we would consider it. The Bench intimated that they had no offer to make and the proceedidgs closed.

Some amusing mistakes made in an essay com• petition on alcohol in Hull are credited to the young essayists in a souvenir which has been lately published. Here are a few of the blunders: "Alcohol is a mocker; at last it biteth like a servant and stingeth like a batter." "To-day mi.ny people are in gaol for committing suicide while under the influence of chink." "A teetotaller is strong and has a better chance to get on in life, whilst a drunkard is weak and fat, and stands at street corners all his life." "It causes liver complaint and consumption, and cities and nations are much more fatal. Shortened lives have been increased." "It ruins many of their families and diseases on the stomach, liver, and consumption." " Alcohol is a dreadful poison which is the root of evilness."

THE WARRINGTON MUSICAL SOCIETY'S NEXT OPERA. lolanthe.

Our readers will be interested to hear that the War- rington Musical Society has now made complete arrangements for its fourth amateur operatic enter- prise, the work chosen being " lolanthe "—one ai the most pipular of the Gilbert-Sullivan series ' operas. This is to be performed on a magnificet scale in Parr Hall on six nights commencing Monday October 15th, and a repetition of the success whicl has attended previous ventures may be confidentl predicted. It is pleasing to note that the Sock intends making an effort to further that most deserv, ing object—the Infirmary Extension fund. The nr proceeds of Monday's and Friday's performances wi be devoted to this instituCon and the remainder will as hitherto, go to the funds of the Musical Society. Wf understand that the rehearsals, already far advancer have been resumed this week after the vacatic holiday, and the enthusiasm of all concerned is most gratifying. " lolanthe " is an exceedingly pretty fairy opera and the spectacular effects will be very fine. As in the past, the training of the opera company is in the joint hands of Mr. F. H. Crossley, as Conductor and Musical Director, and of Mr. Thornley-Dodge as Stage Manager and Dramatic Instructor. Appended is a list of the Dramatis Persona, :

THE EXAMINER. SEPTEMBER 6th 1.906.

The officials of the Beautiful 'Warrington Society will be pleased to receive donations towards the cost of last Saturday's show which was considerable. They are looking forward to a large increase of membership now that the public have had an oppor- tunity of seeing the good work which is being done. * * * The average golfer is a self-contained, self-important being with a figure that runs to fat. His one desire is to keep down his proportions and to render himself, as the phrase goes, fit." To that end he joined an an association of other self-important individuals and goes gallivanting after a ball on blasted heaths and other parts of the kingdom unfit for really useful purposes.--T. M. Rendle in" London Opinion." * * * The increasing abstemiousness among the well-to-do in this country is a matter that deserves more atten- tion than it is receiving. The fact is that since steam has enabled most to move freely, the old have hundreds of amusements which those of their age in former times had not, and they cling tenaciously to life. The old of the past had little left to enjoy but drink ; those of our day dread it as a poison which will shorten life.—" ILarmaduke " in the "Graphic." * * * They were a party of four —a robust looking clergy- man, his wife, and two strong boys, writes " J.H. ' to the " Manchester Guardian" I seemed to spend the whole of Bank Holiday in their company, yet—such is our English custom—we never spoke. We travelled together in the morning, he full of animal spirits, she quiet and somewhat depressed, as if expecting little pleasure from the day's outing. I saw him later in the Cathedral. The mother was already tired. She rested on a chair while the father and the boys ex- plored the fabric. I met them again in the fierce heat of the afternoon at --, a place of general resort a ' few_ miles away. By this time she was an obvious drag on the party, and came along several yards behind the others with a look of weariness on her face. My last sight of them was on the road back to the station. The motor-'bus service had broken down, and we all had to walk. The father and the two boys came on at a swinging pace, chatting gaily together. The mother was now a hundred yards behind. Her bonnet had fallen to the back of her head and the coat on her arm was trailing in the dust. Her breath came fast and her eyes pro- truded as she staggered along in her effort to keep them in sight. As I entered the city the newsboys were shouting " Women victims of cowardice "—but that was on board the Sirio.

152 71 170 112 COMMERCIAL FAlLURES.—According to " Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the total number of Commercial failures recorded in England and Wales during the week ending Friday, August 24th, was 146, viz. :—New Bankruptcy Proceedings published in the London Gazette, 80; Deeds of Arrangement registered 68. The respective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were: Bankruptcies, 73; Deeds of Arrangement 75—total 148; being la decreaselof 2. The total num- ber of Commercial Failures recorded during the 34 weeks of the present year was 5,511 ; the total number recorded in the corresponding 34 weeks of last year was 5,894, showing a decrease of 373.

especially another political party. Now the real point atissue is as to whether our purely labour organizations are to be made to serve the ends of one political party and that the Socialist party. Trade Unionism has built up its great strength by keeping party politics outside and in the interests of labour generally, it is essential that its vitality is not sapped by political intrigues. The Independent Labour Party is an avowed political party. What is prac- tical in its teaching is part and parcel of Liberalism, some of the most extreme items of their programme having been advocated by sections of the Liberal party long before the I.L.P. was born. Se don't complain of the I.L.P. adopting Liberal measures. On the contrary we think it reflects to their credit : but it does seem puerile to endeavour to make people believe that Liberals are opposed to the very items of reform which they have adopted from Liberalism. What is not practical in 1.L.P.-ism but idealistic is Socialism and this the T.L.P. are quite welcome to, although I know of nothing that need exclude idealists from the Liberal party—indeed the ideal whicJa has the strongest influence in the I.L.P. movement to-day was not generated within that party but is the outcome of Liberalism. There is therefore absolutely no necessity for the I.L.P. ; still if it elects to exist we shall do our best to prevent its taking ambush in our labour organisations. While the movement appeared to be genuinely in favour of labour, the help of the Progressive party was given unstintingly for Labour candidates. Since the general election we have had in Cockermouth an example of the true spirit of the I.L.P. and it behoves the Labour party in the interests of progress to assess it at its proper value.—Yours truly, JOHN MORTON, Hon. Sec. (Progressive Labour League).
